WOMEN'S CHAT.

London Exhibitions.—London as ill rejoice in _numerous exhibitions this summer, of bolls a popular and instructive kind. The ever-popular Earls Court Exhibition is already open, and makes a special feature of the Golden West and of things American this season. The White City, the scene of the Anglo-French .Exhibition of last year, is to 'open next week, and will be called the Impeiial International Exhibition. These are the two principal attractions which the public patronise more for the amusements they offer, than for intellectral entertainments and from this point of view the White C;ty promises to excel far beyond any- thing yet seen in London. Special exhibitions are to be devoted to sports naval and military display, and for the next two weeks Prince's Skating Rink is to be given up to a Woman's Exhibition. Man has long delighted in making a show of his industries, and, after thousands of years devoted to cooking, weaving and housekeeping, woman is beginning to follow suit. Her principal effort in this direction will be concentrated on the" Women of all Nations" Exhibition, which is -to be held at Olympia next Septen;ber. It is to be free -from trumpery displays, and will be a comprehensive exhibition, showing how the sphere of womens work lass increased by leaps and bounds daring tbe last twenty or thirty years. A. Costly Duel.—lt is often said that the domestic servant problem would be less acute if household work, instead of being regarded as ornmonplace drudgery, could be restored to its former dignity. There are many people inclined to enthrone woman on a wash- tub, with a broom in one hand, and a darning needle in the other, but there wcs never much disposition to regard the arts and crafts of the horceas other than menial offices. There is a story about the late Earl of Rosebeiy which serves to illustrate this. The late Earl fought a duel with a non-commissioned officer of the French Army, Who had become a haler, was after- wards very anxious that his antagoniat should not go back to his household duties. So, to save biinself from the reproach of having fought with a servant he settled sin annuity of £250 on the. map, on condition that he did not resume domestic service. It was a costly duel to the Earl, as the ex-butler had to keep his part of the bargain for nearly forty years. Lord and Lady Roberts. Not only the Army but the whole Empire hold Lord Roberts in affectionate re,.eard, L and many will be the congratulations offered to ord and Lady, Roberts on the celebration of their golden -wedding on May 17th. Lord Roberts. had already dis- tinguished himself in the Indian Mutiny before his marriage, and it was in fact, whilst on his honeymoon in Scotland, that be was sent for by our late Queen, to receive the Victoria Cross—one of the first to be bestowed as the decoretion "For Valour" was only founded in 185 G. Lord Roberts is now seventy-six, but be is comparatively- young and active ler his years and never ceasss from urging on the nation the necessity' for universal military training, whilst Lady Roberts has done much to promote temperance and nursing organisations in the army. Lord and Lady Roberts have had six children, and the great grief of their life was the loss of their only son who was killed in the 'South African war. They have now only two daughters . . .

===== 0002601_19090515_art0008.txt | included=true | included =====
t`te elder, Lady Aileen Roberts, being heiress to her fathers title. llokeins Pictures.—Windsor Castle contains several specimens of Holbein's art, the painter of the portrait of Christina of Denmark—now tomporprily lent to the National Gallery—which was recently sold by the Duke of Norfolk for £61,000. 'Ono of the Holbein's patrons was King Henry VIII for wham he executed several commissions, and when the King was a disconsolate widower, after the death of Jane Seymour, be sent Holbein to Brussels to.paint for him the portrait of the young Christina of Denmark. That picture is pro- bably the one at Windsor Castle, and not the master- piece which the Duke of Norfolk has parted with, and which is in danger of Icing lost to the nation. There is also at Windsor the portrait which Holbein painted in 1539 of the then Duke of Norfolk, but the picture which has been sold is regarded as the masterpiece, a unique specimen of the art in which Holbein excelled. The Derby Ball.—The Royal ball on Derby night, May 25th, at which the King will he present, will be given by Lord and Lady Farquhar at their honse Grosvenor-square. The Derby ball was for several -years given at Devonshire House, during the lifetime of the late Duke of Devonshire, but Lady Farquhar gave it last year. The Queen will honour Lotd and Lady Farquhar with her presr-nca at dinner, sod the King, who entertains the members of the Jochey Club to dinner at Buckingham Pdlace on perby night, will follow on with many of his friends afterwards. The ball is to witness the debut of the eldest of their Majesties' grandchildren, the Princess. Royals elder daughter—Princess Alexandra—who will be eighteen on the 17th of this month. The Duke of Fife and Lora .Farciehar have always been great friends, hot it is doubtful if the Princess , Royal- will chaperon her daughter at the ball. Quid l and retiring by nature, the Princess Royal has never cared for the-glitter of Courts, and she has avoided Society life in London as much as possible since her health gave way. A New Form of Trimreing.—Queries de rat, or rat- tail, nas to some considerable extent taken the place of braid at the tailors, during the east vk eek or two. The now trimming consists of supple satin rolled over cord, and upon a tracery of the pattern desired, this is sewn. The sewing process is a sornerch4t more difficult one than that experienced in the matter of braids great shill and care being necessary, otherwise the effect is " amstrarrish " and clumsy.

Shoulder Scarves.—The newest scarves, which later oa are destined to take place of the always becoming feather boa, are almost shawl-like dimensions—and carried out in silky Spanish, or blonde lace. These are being finished with a fringe often measuring a quarter of a yard in length (rade of the narrowest ribbon. The scarf, to be correct must exactly mach the toilette with which it is worn, contrasts in this direction being deemed impossible.

Tan i.nd Grey Gloves.—At once lime no modish woman considered herself properly dremed tildes she wore white gloves, whether in the morning, afternoon, or ev..ein4.7,. But this season again, the aite glove— save with the all-white toilette—is conspicuous by its absence. the suede glove in a delicate f.had e of tin or grey, which ever harmonises best m ith the costume, being chosen by the well-dressed woman. Elaborate Notepaper Stamping.—Notepaper stamping is an intricate matter, and quite half a sheet is frequently given up t 3 imparting information both necessary and unnecessary now-a-days. For example, a tiny engine is often portrayed before the name of- bbo station, a couple of teleg!opli poles and wires before the telegraphic address, whilst a receiver calls atten- tion to the telephone; and a steamboat to the nearest pier. Then there is generally a map of the surrounding roads, with a motor en route, the monogram, name or number of the house etc., etc., all of which take up considerable room and form quite a picture gallery. How to Remove Stains.—No woman who cares for her clothes should ever be without that useful conn. modity, fuller's earth, when oily, muddy stains are con- cerned—and these promise to be quite common troubles until the aeroplane removes all motor traffic to the clouds. A mixture of the earth :Ind boiling watur, or the earth and eau-de-Cologne, fairly thin; spread upon the marks will . speedily remove them, that is, if the paste is allowed to dry, and remain on for a day or two. For simple mud stains, a capital procedure is to allow them to become quite dry, then brush well, end finally go over the offending parts with a- cloth wrung out of warm water in which a little carbonate of soda has been dissolved.

SIR JONI: BRUNNER AN

Lord Derby was installed Chancellor of Liverpool University on Sa,turday,and a number of ilittstrouS men were admitted to honorary degrees. Mr. A. J. arlfour, Mr. Birrell, Lord Roberts, Lord Crewe, Lord Charles Beresford, Sir .7. 'l'. Branner, Bart., M.P., and Mr. Marconi were among the recipients. The ceremony took place in St, George's Hall, and as of a brilliant and memorable character.

Sir John Brunner, upon whom was conferred the degree of ",L.L.D," was described as stintding second to noLe in the rinks of "masters in industry." Reared in this city in a hcme that reflected the enlarging influence of knowledge, he had furnished forth a con- spicuous and convincing proof that the path to industrial prosperity lay through the strong alliance of scientific research and business enterprise. A lover of peace, be had proved that peace had her victories as well as war. As repiesentative of the people in Parlia- ment, as member repeatedly of Royal commissions; and 'as Privy' Councillor, he had p!aced his large and matured judgment at the service of his cauntry. -Education had been his constant And absorbing care, and among his many splendid benefactions none stood out more conspicnons than those which hid been directed by a discriminating gen' rOsity to open the ways for the sona and daughters of the people to the higher levels of knowledge, self-help, and good citizen- ship. Nor did they fail to recall with abiding gratitude how much the University of Liverpool owed ' to his great and generous qualities of mind and character—his, muni.ficence to all its faculties, to his enheartfning encouragement in all its enterprises, his untiring sympathy with its te!:ching staff, his voice in its councils, and not least to his faith in its future. A banquet was afterwards held at the Town Hall, when speeches were made by Mr. Balfour, Lord Charles Bet esford, Lord Crewe, Mr. Birrell, Sir J. T. Brunner, Mr. Marconi, and others.

===== 0002601_19090515_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
OVERCROWDING VEGE rABLES.

Growth at this season, if the weather be favourdble, is very rapid, so that everything soon outgrows its allotted space. Plaits of every kind will soon spoil unless they are thinned out from time to time as growth proceeds. This is particularly so with the kitchen garden crop, as they are more apt to get neglected than some of the utthers. Thinning ought always to be reorted to !Afro() there are the least signs of over- crowding.—" Gardening Illustrated."

[By Redcap.]

DUCK FARMING.

is one of the most profitable branches of poultry farming in general. Also it is far easier to raise ducklings than chickens wheir the first few days are over after hatching, and I fail to understand how it is that duck raising is not more extensively gone in for. Some people think that free access to a pond is neces- sary in raising ducklings. This is not so, and while everyoie knows that it is quite natural for ducks to be in water, you must remember that very many natural tendencies have to be substituted by artificial means in producing quick growing stock for table purposes. To build up a sound and lasting constitution in ducks or anything else the laws of nature must be obeyed. But to produce a duckling fit for . the table in ten weeks, methods qf forcing have to be resorted to. I want therefore to assist my readers with a few practical hints how to raise ducklings for the market and also how to raise and keep ducks for egg produc- tion. First of all, it is very necessary that you should have some idea of the

BD7 VARIETIES

to go in for. There are a great many, but most of them need not be mentioned in this article as I purpose to recommend only those breeds best adopted for utilits- purposes. The white or Aylesbury Ducks head the list of our domestic ducks for general purposes for, beside carrying n great amount of flesh meat of a delicate flavour,thev are good layers of a large egg. Formerly they bore the name of English White, but I suppose after finding the neighbourhood of Aylesbury so well adapted for duck raising, that name became associated with the variety. Like many other varieties of ducks and fowls they carry the name of the place where mostly bred and develoPed. Then we have the Chinese-or Pekin ducks imported into this country more than n quarter of a century ago. This also, is a useful variety f. ,r culinary purposes and a very good layer. The Cayuga duck' is another useful variety but not so exten- sively bred as the Aylesbury and Pekin. I suppose because of its colour, which is a greenish tinted black. This is only prejudice for the Cayuga duck is very useful, growing to a large size and the quality of the flesh is excellent. I need only mention one other variety to complete the list of the best breeds for table purposes and that is the Rouen duck, a grand breed and one .that I should specially recom- mend for growing on 'after the first batch or two of early market ducklings. They take longer to mature and if fed on the right lines they make very tine 'table birds at five or six months old. In colour they resemble the wild duck and are much valued as an exhibition bird in England. There is still however another breed that I must not pass over. That is the

INDIAN RUNNER DUCKS,

No one would claim for this variety anything else but egg production, and undoubtedly they are wonder- ful layers and many good records stand to their credit. They are a very fascinating variety; the colour is of a lightish brown and White, and the shape of the bird very much resembles the old-fashioned soda-water bottle,and very often described as such in the show pen. They are good foragers and .if allowed a free range will procure most of their own food. As I have stated. they are not:good table-birds, therefore I should 'advise that they be kept where a good clean pond is available and where they could range over a'meadow for their food. It kept Under such conditions they will be found a most profitable breed for egg production. It is a very common occurence .that when the hens are not laying the Indian Runners are; and where a good market for duck eggs is obtainable I should strongly recommend the Indian Runner, as the bet possib!e breed for that purpose.

===== 0002601_19090515_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
RAISING DUCKS

in large numbers is best done by artificial incubation, because both tune and Money is saved rather than using broody hens. The period of incubation is from 28 to 30 days and the temperature need not be so high as for the incubation of hen eggs, especially now the season is advanced and the days are warmer. As the ducklings hatch out dry them off and place them into the warm brooder and gradually harden ,off .before placing into the cool-brooders, Where large numbers are not required I would recommend .that yol.l should purchase the ducklings, inTact lam inclined to believe that the best system to. adopt is this one. I Should. procure one or two warm and cool-brooders and send for the ducklings direct from one of the many breeds who make a speciality of this section of poultry farming, and thus periodically renew the stock as they grew up and are killed off. On their arrival carefully examine and if any have got crushed or unduly trampled on by the others in transit, separate at once and by careful management such can often be brought round. Of course any dying on rail are always replaced by the vendor. Then as to

===== 0002601_19090515_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
FEEDING

After the ducklings are a few days old they will feed well, and as a rule :cause very little trouble. Care should be taken to feed regularly and often for‘the first few weeks and the food shotrkl consist of boiled rice, oatmeal; and bullock's liver chopped very fine. The latter is a good substitute for natural insect food, such s worths and slugs, both of which ducks are very fond of. Alter feeding on this diet for two weeks you,ean give them a mixture of middlings or thirds two parts, barley meal one part, ground oats one part. Add also some boiled vegetables such as cabbage or svede turnip. - Grease in some form should be added Alen feeding for the market, and pure tallow grease Will serve the purpose vell. Grit is also as essential for ducks as for fowls, but to be given in a different manner, as fellows : Place in the bottom of the drinking trough, some small gravel and broken flint. Thus when they go to drink they dabble in the bottom and pick up what :they require as ,instinut decides. Ducklings bred for

TABLE PUPPOSES

should not have free access to water. - If so they do not put on flesh quick enough. They Should be fed in lots of about twenty in a batch and kept most of their time on clean straw in a roomy shed or out house. A good supply of fresh clean drinking water can be given three times daily for the first few weeks and then twice a day. After having supplied •Aem with -sufficient, take the water away se that the floor does not get contaminated and dirty their plumage, for you must not forget the Table of the feathers if kept clean as an addition :to your income from duck farming. With regular attention and proper feedirr• ducklings Should be ready for killing in ten .weeks time from hatching. Be- careful to catch them before growing their second feather, because at this period they soon go back and lose flesh rapidly. They cannot grow feather and flesh at the same time, and if any of the stock are more advanced in this direction let them run on until the feaher is well grown and feed up after- wards.

PLITKING AN) DRESSLYG

is an important item in marketing ducks. At so early an age.as ten weeks the skin of the young duck is naturally very tender and when plucking is apt to tear. A experienced hand can however pluck them rapidly without tearing and I Should advise the novice to get a few lessons from an 'older hand for it is only by experience that these hints can be, acquired. Of course if ,care and time is taken you can be sueeessfUl in clean dressing, but very. often you may ,i` spot a wrinkle ' in a moment by watching an experienced hand and tints save your time. A duckling badly dresSed.looks very unsightly, and not only So, but the value is .reduced. It certainly worth your while. to take pains in thiS'direc- tion until you become more efficient, for you will soon' learn that a badly dressed duckling does not invite customers. Seine people after plucking and cleaning; , dust over with flour, and for a time they look nice ; but when the flour becomes damp, with the Moisture of the fleSh it turns black and makes the bird louk much worse than before. . My only advice is that if you are not efficient take more time, and if you do happen to tear the skin juA lay •it over the bruise and let it remain ;. foz it vill always look preferable to dirty flour. Ducks intended for

EGO VROKCTION

and stock- purposes need not be fed so liberally as , those for table and if you have access to a pond So much the better, Ton will find, that after the morning feed of soft food they will wend their way to the water and very seldom trouble you further than the evening visit, when a feed of grain, such as wheat or oats, should be given. I am certain that if any of my readers who have not already added ducks to the poultry farm, would soon do so if they could realize what a profitable source .of income they were. They are much less expensive to keep than fowls both in food and housing accommodation. When one sees -how they" shovel", up their food this statement may seem contradictory, but being less active-than the

fowls, their food lasts them longer. Ducks kept under proper conditions and judiciously fed are a very profitable adjunct to the industry of poultry farming. Next week I shall write on Poultry for Exhibition.

TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCIL.

The Strike at Messrs. Whyman's.

A fair number of delegate 3 met in the Independent Labour Rooms, Friars' Green, en Tuesday evening to transact the business of the Council. Mr. P. Delaney presided and was supported at the table by Mr. Edward Plinston (secretary).

===== 0002601_19090515_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
EDUCATION OF THE WORKING CEA SSES.

The SECRETARY read a letter from the Keighley District Labour Council, which stated that they bad passed a resolution and begged the Council to plss the same resolution or a similar one, and send it to Mr. Runciman, Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P., and the Warrington Education Committee. The resolution pieced before tbe meeting Was as follows :- 41 his Council, recognising th It the worker's child cannot hope to obtain in the public elementary school in this country, that efficient education which shall equip him to discharge his duties as a citizen, in which such schools have len adequate staff of competent teachers, desires to give its heartiest thanks to Mr. Runciman, President of the Board of Education, for the proposal which he has placed before the country by means of circular 709, under which schools will be reduced in size, any incompetent teachers gradually removed from the schools and th,ir places filled by men and women trained to develop the faculties with which even the poorest child is endowed." Ms) the following resolution: "This Council there- fore urges the President of the Board of E.-fixation to place in the next issue of the Elementary School Code the regulation outlined in the circular above referred to. Assuring you that in such aq action he will have the hearty support of this Council," Mr. SINCLAIR moved that the resolutions be pas3ed. Mr. HALEY, in seconding, said it was the cost that troubled the Government, as Mr. Trevelyan had said when he came to Warrington. People made a great song and demanded eight Dreadnoughts, but did not think of the cost. When it came to the education of the workingman's child the cost was always thought of. If we could afford eight Dreadnoughts, we could afford to have better education.

The SECRETARY and Mr. RICHARDSON supported. The CHAIRMAN took exception to part of the resolu- tion in reference to "incompetent, teachers." They had an efaciPnt staff of teachers in the elementary schools. If the resolution were passed in the form it was written. he was afraid they were going to inflict a great hardship on uncert7ficated teachers. There were hundreds of teachers up and down the country who were ttocertificated, and were competent teachers. The SECRETARY said that if they wanted the children to be thoroughly tr lined in the future,we must have teachers who were certificated. We had some uncertificated teachers in Warrington, but no mcre were being employed in the schools now. He thought the resolu- tion meant that by the Slow process of years the uncerti- fied teachers would gradually become obsolete. The resolution was unanimously passed. MINE INSPECTION.'

. The following rasolul ion was passed awl sent to the irne Minister, and Mr. A. H. Crosfield, M.P. In view of the serious mining accidents which have taken place recently in the British Coalfields entailing such a serious loss of life and untold amount of suffering, we feel the time has arrived when the Government ought to 1:e asked to facilitate the passing into law of an Amended Mines Act, which will embody the principle of a periodical and systernaticil inspe2tion of all mines by mines inspectors' whose duty it should be ta report upon the general conditions of all mines under his luris fiction. Also duo consideration be given to the appointment of inspectors from the ranks of the worke,s, who have a practical I:nowledge of mining. We belleve by the appointment of workers and a system of thorough inspection would greatly assist in safe- guarding the lives of the underground workers."

TEE STRIKE AT WHYMANS.

Mr. A. M. LOGAN (organiser of the Central Iron. moulders' Association), in appealing for funds to help the men who were out on strike at Whyman's Foundry; said it fell very hard upon those who had families, to keep. In explaining their position Mr. Logan said they had been threatened with a reduction, and the justification for that was that some similar reduction had taken place in Scotland some nine months ago. As they were in Warrington they should do as Warrington did, and not what Scotland did, nnless they could copy them when they did better work than they. •Since the strike insult had been aided to injury by the police. The men who were not on ;dispute had been asked to sign as non-unionists or gq on dispute. Mainly they had refused to sign that notice and they had joined the ranks of the strikers. He had sent letters to the papers which had never been pnblished;and that showed that there was very little help they would get from the papers. They had offered to put the matter to any Arbitration Court that might be decided upon, but unfortunately Messrs. Whyruan had taken up the position that they intend to starve the men out. They had practically been bullied by the police • Who had been trying to prevent them picketing. It was not true that they were tryink to close the place; they only wanted the same money as those in England. Mr. HALEY moved tha t they send a ctronlar appeal- ing for funds to all the branches of the Trade Unions asking tor help. Mr. RICHARDSON seconded.

Mr. PLINSTON said that if the statementa made that, night were absolutely true that the police had interfered with the pickating they had no right to do so. It reminded him of Ireland. where the police practically bril-ed the men into rowdyism and then locked them up. He Roped that the Press mould give a report of their meeting that night, and make it known at least that arbitration had been offered by the men. The Trade Unions desired fair treatment, and fair treatment only.

EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

LEGAL STATUS OF THE MOTOR.

EARLESTOWN BLACKSMITH'S LINSUCCESSFI7L

CLAIM.

The adjourned boring of a claim for damages Caine en at St. Helens County Court on Wednediy, in which Robert Pope, blacksmith, of Stanley-street, Larlestown, sued Messrs. Schweppe, Limited. Mr. A. Smith represented plaintiff, whose case was that on March 4th last he was driving from St. Helens to Eailestown. He had bought a horse at St. Helens, which was being led behind a trap. When they were descending a steep slope in Newton-road, Parr, Pope saw Schweppes' motor-waggon approaching him. His horses became restless, so ha signaled to the motor. driver to stop. The motor, however, come on right up to the trap, which was dragged on to the fOotpatb and upset. Piaintill was injured and his horzes and trap damaged, for which he claimed £35.

Mr: Patrick Hastings, for the defence, called wit- nesses, who said that the motor was stopped seventy yards before it reached the trap, but that the led horse at the back of the trap was so restive.tbat it pulled the trap out of its course and upset it. Judge Shand pointed cut that the law had allowed motors on the road. t hey were no doubt an abomina- tion according to the plaintiff and a good many people, but the law bad allowed them.

Mr. Smith—But the law has preserved their position as nuisances.

Judge Shand said he was not speaking of the matter as a legal nuisance but what he meant was that to many horses motors were a terror. Still, the law allowed them as a fact, and they had to get their horses accus- tomed to them. -.The whole of the case was that the motor-driver improperly drove down the hill when he -ought to have stopped; but he pointed out that plaintiff's witnesses said that the motor stopped before it reached the horse.

Mr. Smith said that if his iHononr as against him on the evidence of his own IVitnes?,esile would go no further. •

Judge Shana said be would hear all the evidence; all he wanted was to,get at the bottom of the matter. After bearing further evidence, his Honour said he was satisfied that the defendants were not to blame, and he give .judgment in their favour, with costs.

REI,LIRKABLE INCIDENT IN A CHESHIRE GARDEN.

A remarkable incident was disclosed at Hartford, Cheshire, on Tuesday. night. ,Some, days ago a young owl w.is discovered helpless in a garden, having appar- ently fallen from a nest hidden amongst the ivy of the Old wall The owlet was replaced, but later a second one was found, and both were placed in a comfortable box. Passing the box a few nights later the gentleman who made the discovery tras suddenly attacked by the parent bird, which flew straight from the nest in his face, striking him under the eye with her claw. She then retuined to her nest.

NOTES ON GAMES, ETC.

Cricket.

With brilliant suni-mer-like weather prevailing, cricket enthusiasts had a grand time on Saturday and them were matches in every part of the district, Warrington journeyed to Ashton-under-Lyne and gave a capital account of themselves, but the home eleven were able to make a draw. The Warrington side lacked the services of the Rev. H. Gray, and his place was taken by Mr. J. W. Pearson, also a master of the Grammar school, who displayed such sound form that he should be a decided acquisition to the club. Warrington won the toss and baying first turn at the wicket amassed the handsome total of 185.

B. Silcock is to be complimented upon a notable display. He was at the wicket for one hour and 49 minutes, making 71 in faultless style, and as he came in at a critical stage of the match, the value of his innings can hardly be over-estimated. His chief hits included seven 4's.

Cresswell, the Warrington- professional, also did yeoman service, his innings, which occupied 57 minutes, realizing 37 and including four 4's. J. W, Pearson signalised his debut lw compiling 20, while T. Wright was responsible for 1-7 andJ. Callon for 14. Ashton-under-Lyne had an hour and three-quarters at their disposal and they had made 109 for the loss of 5 wickets when play ceased. The feature of the innings was a fine stand made by F. L. Eaton and P. S. Kershaw, the first-named being caught after knock- ing up 51 while Kershaw carried out his bat with 36 to his credit.

Warrington 2nd entertained the second contingent from Ashton-under-Lyne and this match also ended in a draw. The visitors batted first and amassed a total of 202 for the loss of eight wickets, to which Warrington responded with 87 with seven wickets down, so that the visitors had considerably the better of the argument. The most successful bowler for Warrington was J. Waywell, who captured four wickets for 64 runs, but the fielding of the home side was much at fault.

The Warrington and District League contest ba, now got into full swing. On Saturday St. James' trounced Rylands's to the tune of 148 runs to 42, J. Hallam (36) and H. Constable(32) being the top scorers, Not one of the wire men made double figures.

Latchford Baptists had little difficulty in defeating Walker's Brewery, the margin being 42. A. E. Walker was in grand form uith the bat, and knocked up 26. E. Allen (13) made the best effort for Walker's.

Wilderspool Brewery put up a miserable display against Sankey Wire Mills 2nd. Only two of their batsmen registered a score, and the total was the nice symmetrical figure of 6 ! The Wire Mills had a field day and gave their opponents muck exercise in the, sport of leather hunting. The innings realized 114, G. 11. W. Steel being the highest scorer with 37. Bell took six wickets for two runs.

The Warrington and District Junior League is doing a praiseworthy work in encouraging the young teams. On Saturday Holy Trintiy trilimphed over Appleton and Stretton to the extent of 28. M. Aukland .(17) was the most successful batsman for the visitors, while R.Whitfield contributed 23 for the villagers.

Sankey showed Padgate a thing or two, for they amassed 09 and dismissed their opponents for the odd 9.. Webb (30) and Foster (21) were the highest scorers on the victorious side. The Padgate score was remarkable for the splendid array of " ducks."

Orford disposed of St. Mary's pretty easily, =king 5-1 for the loss of only one wicket while the Saints were all out for 35. Bramhall carried out his

bat after making just as many as the combined efforts of St. Mary's realized- (35), and he also took six wickets for mine runs.

Warrington Secondary School have registered both a defeat and .a victory. They were not quite equal to Leigh Grammar School and were beaten by 28, but they made short work of Bold-street Wesievans who compiled only 37 against the school's "142. Evatis (33) Rigby (31) Amos (23) and Hodgson (20) batted in grand style for the winners. "Mr Extras " was the to scorer for the Wesleyans.

Warrington Municipal Officers entertainel Altrinc- ham Wesleyan 2nd and scored a victory at their expense, S. Jordan and A. Holland batting well for their respective totals of 21 and 15.

Monks, Hall and Co., opened their season on Saturday when they met Haigh 2nd. and an interesting match ensued. The Warringtonians compiled. 94 to which Haigh had replied with 78 for 8 wickets when time arrived. George Cox distinguished himself with a bright innings which realized 32 and was character- ized by good timing and free hitting. The visitors had the advantage in the bowling department.

Sankey Wire Mills visited Weaverham and made a draw with the villagers. Going in first, Weaverham declared their innings at 100, W. Johnson (51 not out) giving a brilliant display. The Wire Mills knocked up 46 for the loss of 9 wickets, W. Woolcock (12) and H. Hawthorn. (10) being the top scorers.

Lyinm administered defeat to Ashton-on-Mersey, the majority being 24. T. Marsh compiled an excellent 41for the winners, while P. Nicholson was in grand form for Ashton, his innings realizing 51.

The Lynun 2nd eleven were less fortunate at Ashton-on-Mersey, the home contingent winning by the narrow margin of 4. Shakeshaft liatted brilliantly and compiled 46.

Grappenhall had Runcorn for their visitors, but failed to gain the victory, the Runcorn men making 68 as compared with their 55. 0. 11. Johnson (21 not out) did well for the winners and J. Pendry knocked up an acceptablo 20 for Grappenhall.

Warrington St. Paul's were beaten by Ditton to the extent of 14 runs. E. Harrison (12) and E. Beesley (11) batted creditably for the visitors, but none of St. Paul's players attained double figures.

Latchford Baptists visited Helsby and suffered defeat by 21 runs. Latchford batted first, but the first few wickets were not productive of many runs. Eventually Wilkinson put a better Complexion on thinp.s with 10 not out. Cliffe batted well .and also score7d double figures, the whole side being out for 03. Helsby replied with 84, of which P. Rivington con- tributed 21. For Latchford J. Cox took five wickets for 14 runs.

Walker's Brewery, who scored a creditable home lictory on Saturday, play Manchester Brewery to-day at Manchester. It is worthy of note that they have yet to suffer defeat in Manchester since they joined the League.

Bowling.

Ideal weather conditions favoured the opening of the Warrington Works Competition and the success which attended the two matches on Saturday would appear to indicate that the tourney will prove to be the best on record. The system of handicapping which has been adopted for the first time should give some of the younger teams a better chance and make the struggle for the cup all the keener. On Saturday Longford. Wire Co., triumphed over It. A. Naylor's, by five points, the teams starting on level terms. There was a tough contest between A. Smart (Naylor's) and J. Shehnerdine (Longford). The latter player led 6-1 but at the tenth end the scores were equal with 7-7. Then Smart found his form and had a run of .succeses. At one stage he was ahegAby 4-7 but Shelmerdine made a great effort and onfe more the players were level with 14 each. Then Smart bowled the winning wood and ran out victor in a well-fought game by the odd point.

Another interesting bit of play was seen between A. Lloyd (Naylor's) and 3. Appleton (Longford). After a run of give-and-take play Appleton got to the fore by 10-8, but Lloyd was alert and pzeling at 11 gained the victory by 15-13.

In the other match C.L.C. were opposedto Fletcher's (No. 2) Moulders, and although the railway men had to concede 25 point they displayed such grand form as to win by .17. There were very few keen games. One of the best was between A. Morrison (Fletcher's) and F. Dale (C.L.C.) For a time Morrison carried all before him and led 12-3. But Tale made a surprising improvement with the result that Morrison was not able to increase his score and was beaten to the extent of 15-12.

Rugby Fo3tb3ll.

It is not necessary to remind members of the fact, but the general public may be interested to know that the annual meeting of the Football Club will be held on Thursday evening next in Bold-street schoolroom. In addition to the reports there is rather a heavy agenda of special business. The six members of the Committee retire, and are eligible for re-election. There are also eight new candidates nominated. In addition there are three special resolutions, one to increase the Committee from six to ten, another six to nine, and a third six to eight.

- There seems to be practically a unanimous feeling in favour of enlarging the Committee and in all probability the third resolution raising the numbers from six to eight will get the most support. When this point has been settled, there will doubtless be -ome close balloting for places on the Committee. Among the eight new candidates are several men who take a keen interest in the doings of the club and likewise possess the capacity for Club management. Personally, I think one or two changes would have a healthy effect, but I trust that the members will see the wisdom of hastening slowly. It would be the height of, folly to go in for anything like a wholesale change. Experience counts for a lot in football club management, and there are men on the Committee to-day whose services could ill be spared. If there are two changes and two additions there will be a quite adequate infusion of the new blood which seems to be required.

I notice that there is a proposal to appoint professional auditors. This would be folly and extravagance. The auditing is done quite satis- factorily now at a nominal sum, and there is no apparent justification either for a change of method or men.

The financial statement of the Club will probably come as a gratifying surprise to most members. If the Club has not won cups it has retained the interest and support of the public. Gate money has increased, expenditure has been watched with a careful eye, and on the year's working there is a clear gain of 03. The assets which are of the solid and substantial kind, stand at the fine figure of A:1,05, with liabilities only amounting to £lOl.

" Well-wisher " writes to voice • Cie claims of the younger generation. He says : " Having watched the athletes of Warrington for a considerable number of years, I think the young athlete does not get his share of sport. There is one sports meeting in Warrington namely the Football Club's. At these sports there is only one race for youngsters, under 14, and none at all for youths from the age Of 14 to IS. Therefore the youths under that age have got nothing .to train for, as they cannot possibly pay fares and expenses to run elsewhere. For all that the boys at Warrington don't lose heart, as a visit to one • of. the training • quarters will show you. Youths between the ages of 14 to 18 being a large proportion of the Rugby Club's spectators, I think it their duty to promote a race for them. There would .be great entry, seeing that several youths won prizes at Cadishead, Frodsham and Croft sports last year."

If he does nbt someone else will. It is a mistake to suppose that employment is at the mercy of individuals. It is created by the wants of the community.

There is no charitable THE object we can think of POOR CHILDREN'S which is deserving of CAMP. more generous support than that which aims at taking poor children out of the slums and giving them a healthful holiday at the seaside. The Warrington Children's Summer Camp is appealing through its Treasurer and Secretary,Messrs. J. A. Ransome and W.H.J. Jenkins, for more money to extend the scope and usefulness of the camp at Prestatyn. We are not in the least surprised. Warring- ton produces poor children at a distressingly rapid rate, and as a community it should not be laggard in providing all the compensations possible for the small sufferers of our imperfect social system with its underpaid, irregular embloyment. The Wairington Camp must never appeal in vain if Warrington people are to retain their self-respect. They have many other duties, but they have none more' urgent and insistent than those which con- cern the welfare of the children of the poor.

Th e Jubilee of the THE VOLUNTEER Volunteer movement JUBILEE. has been celebrated during the week. It was riot, however, until June, 1859, that the movement found support in Warrington. On the 20th of that month a public meeting, called in compliance with a requisition to the Mayor (Mr. T. Glazebrook Rylands), was held in the Old Music Hall to take into considera- tion the expediency of forming a Rifle Volunteer Corps for Warrington and the neighbourhood. The Mayor presided and the room was well filled. Speeches in support of the movement were made by Mr. Clare, Mr. Beamont, Mr. Robert Davies and the Rev. A. A. Bridgman. An amendment against the formation of a Corps was moved by Mr. Edmund Robinson, seconded by Dr. Smith and supported by Mr. Robert Gaskell and Mr. West. The amend- ment was lost and resolutions were passed to the effect that subscriptions be forthwith invited to assist in defraying any expenses that might be incurred, that a committee be formed to carry out the foregoing resolution, and that the meeting pldge itself to use every exertion to-promote the formation of ,a Rifle Corps and to induce volunteers to come forward and enrol themselves. It is now a matter of local history that these efforts proved very successful and that the War- rington Volunteer Corps continued to be one of the strongest and most efficient until its conversion into a Unit of the Territorials.

The large attendance at A LOCAL FISCAL the fiscal debate in DEBATE. th e Thewlis - street

schools on Monday evening was gratifying, because it indicated. that the workingmen of Warrington are taking a keen interest in a: question which so vitally concerns their material welfare. As to the debate, it is due to both protagonists to say that they presented their respective cases in a manner which evidenced close study and a fair grasp of the question 'as it affects the iron and steel industry. There was a tendency to over- burden the speeches with masses of statisti- cal information, but this was excusable in view of the fact that figures must necessarily be largely used, if only as "illustrations." At the same time it is diffieult for the aVerage listener to realize the import of piles of statistics reeled off with the rapidity which the exigencies of time demand. While the debate was keen, Mr. J. Elwell, the Free Trade champion, undoubtedly had the better of the argument and appeared to carry the audience with him in the well-reasoned and .convincing claim which he submitted for the maintenance of the existing system. He was able to show that the Protectionist wail of despondency is not justified by facts, that England is holding her own in the markets of the world, and that chiefly in consequence of the abundant supply of raw material which Free Trade ensures.

The patching of the THE EVOLUTION United States Tariff is OF A TARIFF, following the course which characterizes all such revisions. The party bringing in the tariff appeals at the polls to the general con- sumer and promises a tariff on scientific lines and also that the general good shall stand before that of particular interests. No doubt in this theyrare sincere—more or less. When, however, their tariff takes form it is assailed by all the interests affected and it finally depends on the most powerful trade interests for the form of its evolution. The general consumers are forgotten and ignored because as a body they 4re lacking in cohesion and indifferent. The tariff, there- fore, which begins with big promises and pious intentions degenerates into a miserable Scramble of selfish interests. The "Morning Post;' Washington, writes that "many newspapers are denouncing the tariff bill as a manufacturer's tariff instead of a con- sumer's tariff, which, they maintained, is what the people expected and what the Republican party promised them in the last campaign.

• In the taxation of land TAXING UNEARNED values, Mr. •Lloyd- INCREMENT. George has given as practical a refutation to the suggestion that his measures are vindictive in character, as he .has given to the sUggestion in regard to licensing. As Mr. Haldane pointed out, the one case in which increased taxation might involve hardship—the taxation of agricultural land —has been excluded from the Budget. The owner of agricultural land has to make con- siderable outlay from his nominal income. But the taxation of land values does not fall upon such owners. It falls only upon land- owners who, spending nothing, get a large increase of their income for doing nothing' but obstructing the demand for facilities which a great community 6reates. As in the case of licences, the Budget only insists upon • the tight of the community to recover some portion of its own property. The principle involved in the Budget resolu- tions relating to land is, as Mr. Haldane said, that we should levy a duty upon what people realise or can realise. It is a tax upon what they have got, not upon what they have not got. The duty upon increment value is to be taken when the value is realised—upon the transfer, or a grant of a lease, and on death when the property passes. In the case of the duty on the value of benefits to a lessee -on the determination of a lease, the intention- is simply to levy it upon the.profits. "No profit, no tax." The Budget resolution con-

fiscates nothing, it only asks that the com- munity should have its share in the profits which it creates.

There is something ENORMOUS almost exquisitely PROFITS .FOR unreasonable about the "THE TRADE." attitude of the Opposi- tion to the new liquor duties. They are, if anything, more zealous for the interests of "the trade" than "the Trade" itself. "The Trade" cried out very shrilly before the Budget was introduced. The Trade cried out before it was hurt, and .now it is very doubtful whether it will be hurt at all. Mr. Herbert Samuel has given some significant figures which shoW that " the Trade" in order to meet taxation to the amount of about £4,000,000 a year, has raised its prices to such an extent that it will actually get about £20,000,000 sterling. Prices have been put upon spirits which have not as yet paid a penny of duty. And even ,when the spirit taken out of bond before the Budget is \exhausted, "The Trade" will make an enormous profit. The increased duty is 3s. 9d. per proof gallon. The retailers in many places are putting on an extra id. a glass, which means an increase of ss. 4d. a gallon. This will bring an increased profit to "the Trade" of no less than £2,773,000 a year. The price of beer is also to be raised. If a halfpenny a glass is put upon beer, the increased profit to "the Trade" will be no less than £17,000,000 a year as the result of the Budget proposals. In this respect it must be conceded that the Budget will lead to spoliation, but it is spoliation against which the Government are powerless. And more- over it illustrates the point that Tariff Reformers always challenge—that when taxes are imposed it is the consumer who pays. If "the Trade" are resolved to fleece the public, only the public can prevent it— by abstinence.

-f NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins has been acting up to the city beautiful idea in Stockton Heath by planting the open road-side opposite his house in Alfred-street with shrubs and trees and using his endeavours to persuade his neighbours to follow suit.

r.* - In a local school the other day the boys were given an essay on what occupation they would like to follow, and having made a selec- tion they were invited to give reasons. One boy wrote that he would like to be a joiner "because they were oftenest out of work."

* * At the recent Local .Government Board inquiry into the Corporation's application to proceed with an extensive schenie of private street works, the Inspector was asked to use his influence with the Board to expedite the sanction to the loan, in order that some work might be provided for the unemployed. Evidently this plea had some weight with the authorities for this week the Board have notified their approval of the borrowing of £22,000. . .

• Warrington Bridge has been town's talk for such a long time and forms such a leading feature in the topography of the town that one can scarcely credit the fact that there are some school children who have never seen it or heard of it. But in one of the principal schools this week, the boys were given an essay to write on Warrington Bridge. Eleven confessed to the master that they knew nothing about it, and so far as they knew had never been over it. * * *

Lord Exeter (says the " Liverpool Post ") has written stating that, in consequence of the Budget, all his contributions to charitable institutions are to be reduced by one-half. A " gentleman " writes to the " Times " explaining that he possesses a well-earned income of well over £5,000 a year, "but when the super-tax comes into force he intends to discontinue his charitable subscrip- tions." The new income taxes will decrease his annual income by about £9O, and still leave him the modest competency of £4,910 per annum. These are types of mean men who might well be marooned at the expense of the State and their goods and chattels devoted to some proper use. They apparently believe that a contribution to public charities gives them some moral title to exemption from the ordinary duties of citizens and the obligations of the Christian * * * A correspondent writes: The slip made by the " Guardian " in reporting the Par- liamentary Debating Society's dinner which you quoted last Week was very entertaining. What I have heard described •as an old schoolboy mistake was made at the same dinner by one of the most eloquent of the speakers. He talked about something being anathema maranatha." To say that a thing is anathema is sufficient. Maranatha is merely Aramaic for " the Lord cometh." The words, in the passage of St. Paul's first Epistle to the Corinthians (xvi. 22), come immediately after the words "let him be anathema "—hence the mistake. * * *

The ProgreOive Labour League and the Tariff Reform League have at last got to close quarters on the question of a public debate, after a good deal of fencing and feint- ing—mostly the latter. We are informed that arrangements have been 'made to debate the question : "Would a return to Protection benefit the nation ?" The Pro- gressive champion will be Mr. J. Howes, and the Tariff Reform exponent Mr. Tom Small,. of Burnley, who addressed a meeting in Warrington last week, and made statements which are roundly controverted in our cor- respondence columns. Mr. Smith will need to be more careful of his facts when the time comes to meet Mr. Howes. The latter keeps a bag of nails specially for,false coin. * *

",He was a pattern of honesty, he was of unfailing courtesy, and he had natural gifts of eloquence and humour. Above all he was compassionate towards the poor." Such was the, tribute paid by ,Judge Parry to the memory of Mr. T. F. Byrne, the well-known barrister, of Manches,ter, who* sudden death on Sunday last came as a painful shock to his many friends. Mr. Byrne had frequently taken part in important cases in Warrington and all who were privileged to come into contact with him will readily endorse Judge Parry's words. His most recent appearance here was as the representative of the Public Prosecutor in the election petition proceedings. Mr. Byrne was a convinced Liberal and in 1895 he contested the North-West Manchester division against Sir W. H. Houldsworth. He was unsuccessful, but considering all the circumstances of the contest he did exceed- ingly well.

* * It is, in the ordary event, a popular task which falls to the Opposition when it raises a protest against new or increased taxation. But the-Opposition does not protest against taxation; on the contrary, it would go one ,better than the Government if it were to come into power. What it does protest against is-the allocation of the burden. It wants to see the great mass of the people

more heavily taxed,while the vested interests go free. Some peculiar obsession seems to distort the mental and moral outlook of the Conservative Party, so that they can never conceive of any rights or justice for the people as a whole. They are always willing that the consumer—who is everybody— should pay. They are always consumed with a fire of uprighteous indignation if a comparatively small class of monopolists is asked to shoulder its burden like the man in the street. Thus we see them moved almost to tears at the unhappy plight of the brewers distillers and publicans—who between them are plotting to divide an increased profit of £20,000,000 a year. They have no sympathy to spare for the consumer, who must pay upim his whisky and beer an impost so many times more heavy than that which the Chancellor of the Exchequer levies. * * *

Wa understand that Alderman Bolton has this week been the recipient of an autographed portrait of the' King. His majesty is depicted as a Field Marshall in undress uniform. Alderman Bolton con- siders it a great honour to have received such a •recognition and values the portrait highly.

* The vexed question affecting the position of married women teachers in Warring- ton is gradually coMing nearer to a settlement. The School Management Com- mittee has accepted the reasons given for the retention of three of these ladies as " special " reasons, and decided to con- tinue their services. One teacher concerned has resigned, and we presume the notices served upon the remainder will hold good. Seeing the tendency of Education Authorities all over the country to dispense with married women teachers, the position is hardly worth contesting by those in Warrington who con- sider themselves aggrieved and not dis- missed on "educational grounds."

Death- of Mr. J. Haynes. Many of our readers will regret to 'hear of the death of Mr. James Haynes, proprietor of the Academy- street Cafe, who passed away on Sunday after ten weeks' illness. Mr. Haynes, who was sixty-six years of age, came to Warrington from Northamptonshire about thirty-five years ago and commenced a boot bminess at 44 Horsemarket-street. He subsequently took the adjoining premises and carried on business at both shors for many years, having a leather ware- house in Peter-street. Two years ago lie opened the Academy-street Cafe. Mr. Haynes, who was much esteemed by a large circle of fiends, was a staunch Liberal in politics, but took no part in public affairs. He was formerly connected with Wycliffe Congrega- tional Church. From his youth he bad teen a member of the Order of Oddfellows (Manchester Unity). Some years ago his chief hobby was bird fancying, in which he ook great interest. Much sympathy is felt with the widow and family (three ' sons and four daughters) in their bereavement. The funeral took place at the cemetery on Wednesday, the Rev. John Yonge officiating. Prior to the interment a short service was held at the house. Amongst those present in addition to the ruembet s of the family, were Messrs. William Beswick, George Wallington, E. England, A. Booth, Sudlow, J. Kershaw, Harold Wallington, Wilson (Liverpool), and Cooper (Widnes). TALBOT INVINCIBLE CARS. Trials by !appoint- raent.—Ageat, J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge- st.—Advt.

Warrington Cycling Club. The journey to Hunt's Cross on Saturday last was well attended and carried out under ideal weather conditions. Starting from headquarters early -in the afternoon, with the assistance of a favourable wind a pleasurable passage out was made. The Bowling Green at the destination was in excellent condition and members made the mcst of it and the weather by spending the best part of their stay with the "woods.' A repast of the nature familiar %%lilt the W.C.C. members served at the Hotel was amply appreciated by the " boys " after' their exertions. The ride home proved eventful by an epidemic of punctures, and frequent halts and•calls were made for the official repairers who with the aid of numerous lamps did yectean service and Warrington was reached in good time after an enjoyable run, with memories not altogether pleasant of the Lancashire roads. The second bowling meeting of the season at Sankey on Wednesday evening was well attended, but on account of the heavy rain during the day the green was not so suitable for bowling as might have been desired. The tun to Arley and Great Buthvorth on Thursday evening took place, but-owing to the adverse weather a number of the regular mid-:week riders were absent. There was a good muster however, and the run prcved successful. The Club arrangements for the current week are :--To-day's run to Chester, Wednesday evening, bowling at Sankey, Thursday, run to Preston Brook and Little Leigh.

The Empire Day celebrations at Warrington on Monday, May 24th, will be on a more elaborate scale than hitherto, for the senior children will be assembled on the lawn in front of the Town Hall, where they will be addressed by his Worship the Mayer and by the Chairman of the Education Committee. The Mayor will present a medal to the boy and a 'brooch to the girl of each senior department who, by the vote of their fellow scholars, are adjudged to be worthiest of the honour. The proceeding 3 will close with the National Anthem.

Gallant Rescue from Drowning.

A most heroic rescue from drowning was made on Tuesday night from the old river, running alongside Greenall's-avenue at Stockton Heath. It appears that an infant aged three years, na'med John Harmer, of Malmesbury-place, Stockton Heath, was playing on the banks of the river with some other children when he fell into the water and was soon afterwards seen struggling 'in the centre. The children screamed and attracted a number of people to.. the banks. One man attempted a rescue, but being unable to swim he failed. 'Happily the boy's father's cousin, Robert Harmer by name, residing near the spot at 6 Greenall's Avenue, was sitting at his door and heard the children's screams. Rushing to the scene and seeing only the child's hands above the surface of the water, he jumped into the river and brought the boy safely to the bank. The child was immediately taken to the rescuer's house, and after a while he was brought round. He passed a bad night but is now running about as usual. The rescuer has

_ previously saved three children from d rowning and also stopped a runaway horse. WESLEYAN METHODIST DISTRICT

SYNOD.

The meetings of this Synod have been held at Southport As a Church, Methodism glories in the brotherliness of its ministry and the devotion and loyalty of its laymen. 'On Tuesday one hundred and forty ministers, and an Wednesday the same number, reinforced by an army of one hundred and seventy laymen, met to discuss the year's work. The subject which aroused the greatest interest was the revision of the conditions of membership.—After four hours' debate it was resolved that inasmuch as the Synod was sharply divided upon every point that the question be left to the judgment of the Conference.

The —meetings were characterised by a hopeful spirit. The reported decrease of members, whilst deplored, was not regarded as a sign of a weakened hold upon the people, but as due largely to ur.settled methods of enumeration. Liverpool district—which includes Warrington —reports an increase of 205 members for the year. Membership returns show 24,944 names; the adherents are about five tireee that number. Foreign Missionary net income amounted to £14,771.

I ethodism has a great hold upon the life young of the District as is evidenced by the vigour of the Sunday schools. Fifty-nine thousand young people and nearly seven thousand officers and teachers meet weekly for Bible study.ln addition to these there are 5,500 meeting in Brbtherhoods and P.S A-.'s. Wesley Guilds show a membership of 7,812 The continued activity of the Church in this district is seen in the erection of new, and alteration of the present,chapels at an estimated cost of £50,626. The Trust property in this district s'iows an annual income of £42,000. There 4re twelve candidates for the ministry. Methodists in Warrington will rejoice that Mr. G. B. Keeling, son of the Rev. E. B. Keeling, passed the examinations very creditably. It is hoped that he will be equally successful before the July CSlntnittee and Conference. Warrington was represented at the gatherings by all the circuit ministers along with Dr. Bennett, Messrs. F. A. Harris, E. J. Guest, 8.A., J.P., J. C. Holland, and J. G. Withinshaw.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

" East Lynne."

The stage version of Mrs. Henry Wood's famous novel has enjoyed a popularity almost as striking as the book itself, and large audiences have assembled at the Royal Court Theatre this week to follow anew the sorrowful 'story of Archibald Carlyle and LOT Isabel. The play, which is exactly the same as presented by the late Mr. Wilson Barrett and Miss Heath, is powerful adaptation of the novel, all the most telling incidents being preserved, and it makes a strcing appeal to the emotions. Mr. Tom Watt's company gives a very finished performance, and those who wish to see "East Lynne" admirably played should not miss this opportunity. Mr. Edmund Kennedy, who has previously achieved conspicuous success in Warrington as "Lucky Durham," gives a natural and sympathetic pertrayal of, Archibald Carlyle and is rewarded with rounds of applause. Miss Julie Kennard does full justice to the dual role of Lady Isabel and Madame Vine, which calls for dramatic skill of a high order. Miss Kennard,however, has made a careful study of the part and is fully equal to its exacting demands, her acting being characterized by an earnest- ness and intensity which never fails to grip the audience. Mr. Cecil Elg,ar as Francis Levison is semi-comic villain, but his preseitment is exception- ally clever and proves him to be a talented artiste. One of the chief reasons of the popularity of this version of" East Lynne "is that it is the only one containing the character of P.C. Bullock. This amiable custodian of the peace is impersonated by Mr. Joe Rowland who has the audience convulsed with mirth during his brief interludes. Mr. Cyril Hayward as Lawyer Dill and Mr. Frederick Jacques as the violent-tempered Justice Hare are also a comical pair, and Miss Lilian Mason portrays Cornelia Carlyle, ,Archibald's masterful sister, in a really skilful style. Miss May Heron is an attractive Barbara Hare and all the other parts are well played.

NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME.

Next week Mr. and Mrs. F. G. Kimberley, Travis Green and Arthur Hinton's Company will present " Was she to Blame ? " written by Mrs. F. G. Kimberley, who will herself take the leading part. The play is a powerful one, dealing with the Church and the stage. Love and passion, vengeance and forgiveness, avarice and sacrifice, wit and frolic are in turn exemplified, and it is claimed that the play will keep the audience in thrilled interest from beginning to end.

The following resolution was passed :—" Ibis meeting of the South-West Lancashire Federation of Free Church Councils desires to express its sincere thanks to the Prime Minister for introducing into the House of Commons a Bill for the Disestablishment of the Church of England in Wales. It recognizes in the • Bill an eainest effort to meet the just demands of the Welsh people for religious equality; and bopes the Government will do all in its power to pass the Bill into law at the earliest possible moment."

THE HALFPENNY TAX ON IDLE

TOWN LAND.

Striking Illustration From Widnes.

From hundreds or perhaps thougands of similar cases we take one (says the" Daily sews ") to show the necessity for such a tax as this. Sir Jobn Brunner, speaking in the House of Commons on Februtry 10, 1899 sAid : "The centre of Widnes belonged to a family whose fortune was founded upon the purchase of land in Lancashire which, at the time of the death of the grandfather of the present heid of the family, was of such little value that his two sons doubted whether it was worth while to prove the will and claim the pro- perty. . . Now that property is worth very decidedly over £3,000,000 and the owners of it have never from beginning to end, paid a penny in rates." If those owners had been holding their land at only £2,000,000 for ten years under a halfpenny land tax, they would have paid £lO,OOO to the community whose industry is the sole cause of that accumulated value. If they preferred not to pay £l,OOO a year for the privi- lege of continuing their speculation, the Exchequer's loss would be the great gain of Widnes in every' possible way.

WHITE CITY RE-OPENING.

To-day (SAturday) the Manchester White City begins its third season of catering to the taste of the public for outdoor entertainment and as the reputation of this popular resort ii already established for presenting only the cleanest and most wholesome amusement public confidence is already assured. For the 1909 summer a number of very extensive improvements have been made in the grounds and new and beautiful buildings erected, among there being a portion of the immense Exhibition palaces that will cover several acres of the White City before the end of the present year and where all the principal exhibitions of Man- chester will in future be held. The ens big building just- completed and opened to-day for the first time will be used as a Roller Sleiting rink and it will be. the largest in Europe, covering more than 40,000 square feet of space and giving the finest maple floor in England. Admission to the White City with an ordinary day ticket includes the use of the skating floor for one of the daily sessions, or the visitor if he prefer*. can get three coupons value one penny each at a number of shows, etc. A magnificent new Band Stand has been erected in a specially laid out garden, where the White City Band as well as the band of 11. M. Irish Guards will be heard in a ten hours' concert every day from 1 to 11 p.m. A great pyro-dramatic spectacular production called" Fighting the Flames "and employ- ing more than 100 men, horses, etc, will be a thrilling new feature, while Gillenos Circus, Miss Ella's Famous Tiained Lions and Madame Collard's Dog Menagerie and a dozen other novelties will be seen, and all the old favourite riding devices and other devices will be retained. The White City is greatly improved and furnishes a maximum of pleasure with a minimum of cost to all who visit it. Sacred concerts are to be given free to invited guests every Sunday ofternoon.

A CARTER'S CLAIM.

At the Borough Police Court on Tuesday, Robert Marshall, 4, Challenor at., was the applicant for £1 4s. 6d. wages, resulting from a breach of contract by Messrs. John Buckley and Co., Ltd., Church-street.— Applicant stated that he came to Warrington on the 15th February. He went to respondents' officers, and saw Mr. Muskett, managing director. Applicant asked him for employment telling where he could apply for references if needed. It was agreed, applicant alleged, that be should start at a guinea a week. He stated the next day (Friday.)On the 26th of March,on arriving early in the morning he mas ti)ld that he bad not cleanei his horse over night and he was not to start work until he bad sgvn Mr. Muskett. He waited about until 9 o'clock and then slim Mr. Muskett, who also said he had been told that applicant did not clean his horse, and thereby he would have to dismiss him at once. Applicant asked Mr. Muskett for a week's wages in lieu of a meek's notice and he would not give it to him saying it was not customary. Applicant said he would have to con- test the case in Court as he was only sticking up for his right. Where applficant came fronn,it was customary for employes and employes alike to gives week's notice. —ln answer to Mr. Taylor, solicitor, who defended, applicant said he hae never been employed in War- rington befere. Ho had been employed carting hay, straw and furniture. He told Mr. Muskett that he. had been employed by a hay and straw dealer. He admitted that be only asked Mr. Muskett for a day or two's work. He denied asking Mr. Muskett afterwards if be would emp!oy him regularly as he %anted to remove his family to Warrington. Applicant denied neglecting the bors.e.—Mr. Taylor, in his defence, said that Mess's. Buckley Ind Co., being furniture dealers, were always very busy at that time of the year, and always employed extra men. When trade got a bit slack they dismissed them. On the 15th February the applicant visited Mr. Muskett and asked him for a job as he had been tra.np- ing looking for work. Nothing at all was mentioned about permanent labour. On the 25th of March applicmt bad to do some carting between Partington and Padgate and at seven o'cleck he came in, put his horse in the stable and left without clean!ng or brushing it down.—A horse keeper (Alf. Jewett) was told to brush the horse down. The next morning applicant was dismissed for neglecting to do his work.—Mr. Muskett, John Muskett, jun., and All Jewett corroborated Mr. Taylor's statements.—The case was dismissed.

THE FEEDING OF SCHOOL CHILDREN.

At the Warrington Police Court on Tuesday before Alderman Bolton (in the chair) and Messrs. C. Jackson Holmes and W. T. Williams, Patrick Kilduff, 34, Varburton-street, was summoned on a claim for 10s. 2d., the cost of food supplied to his two children under the Education (Provision of Meals) Act, 1906.— Mr. A. T. Hallaway (deputy town clerk) said the case had been brought under section 2 of the Act by which parents who were in a position to pay for the meals supplied by the authority were to be charged with the costs. .1 he Education Committee were of opinion that the children were not able to receice a proper education owing to lack of nourishment. The consequence was that the children were provided with food by the Education Committee at a cost of 10s. 3d.—Mr.J.Moore Murray (director of education) said the committee were of opinion that the father was able to rrovide the necessary food.—James Quinn proved that the defend- ant was earning 4s. or ss. a day, but Mrs. Kilduff sad that. only 3r. Gd. a week was given to her by her hustand.—An order was made for the payment of the amount at the rate of a shilling a week.—A similar case against John Mahenson, 62, Dallam-lane, failed .owin.7 to the prosecution being unable to prove that defendant was able to pay. The charge was of neglect- ing to pay 12s. 2s. for meals provided for his three children.

ThOaBth Sunday school anniversary was held on Sun- day, the morning service being held in the chapel, and the afternoon and evening services in Messrs. Cr o, field's Dining Hall. Prior to the morning service the scholars paraded several streets in the district, s'nging hymns at various stages. The preacher was Mr. J. W. Deakin, of Northwich, whose sermons were much appreciated. His morning discourse was on "Service,' in which he showed the delights and anticipations of the teacher, and urged for a full consecration of mind and energy to the work of the Master. At the evening service the preacher portrayed the great life work of Christ, in which he showed that the Saviour came "not to he ministered unto, but to minister." This was the work of the true teacher, and he appealed to all erlgsged in the training of the child to emulate the true spirit of the great Master Teacher. In the afternoon a musical service was held. Special music was rendered by the children and choir. Mr. T. Hale (senior superintendent) %yes the chairman, and in the course of a short address, spoke on the general work of the mission. The Rev. F. Vaughan, of Emmanuel Church, was the principal speaker. He directed his remarks to the inspired work of teacher and parent. The teaching of the Gospel of Christ to tho child was the most glorious duty men and women could be engaged in. It was of utmost importance to-day to keep the children from the various evils surrounding them, and prepare them for the struggle which lay in stere. All workers should be filled with an inspired zeal to win the child for the Saviour whom they served.—Special hymns andanthems were rendered most effectively at morning and evening services by the chilaren and choir, and reflected great credit on the conductor, Mr. F. Atkinson. The singing was accompanied by the St. Barnabas' Orchestral Society, while Mr. F. Tanner presided at the organ. The con- gregations throughout the day were very large. The collections, in aid of the schosl funds, amounted to over 216.
