Wycliffe P.S.A. Annual Meeting.

Another year in the annals of the Wycliffe P.S.A. has passed and on Sunday last the various annual reports wers presented to the members of the Society. The Rev. John Yonge presided over a good attendance. Mr. BENJAMIN FARRAR in presenting tho rith annual report of the Society, said that although their numbers were not large they had been a little steadier during the last year. The spirit and influence of their meetings had been equal to any previous year. In the election of the committee that day, let them try to elect men who had the deepest desires for the success of their work. They should elect men with ideals ' • men who had the courage to push ren to the front, men with the spirit and mind of Christ, who sought not their own, bur. the good of others. They had during the past-year confined themselves to local speakers, who had been very acceptable. Their fears that their local men would not please their members bad been banished. They hoped to continue the same this year. Of course they did not disparage visitors from other districts, and they would be glad to see as many of them as their friends would allow. They were grateful to all those who had so willingly helpei to make the services, bright, cheerful and happy. They heartily thanked Mr. Payton, their organist, for all he bad done and for the vocal and instrumentalist friends be had got for their services. They were also very grateful to the various officials, who had been constant in their work. He expressed thanks to the" Markers," and to the various secretaries who had done their very needful work. The officers of the Sick Club had done well, and were able to pay a dividend of 4s. at the close of the year. They were hoping for a good increase of members this year. The officer's of the sayings club would have been better pleased if more of the brethren had placed their savings in their hands, and they too were hoping for a larger number of depositors. They were pleased that the call upon the benevolent secretary had net been larger, yet he was sure the brethren would, as they had aleays done, respond cheerfully and liberally. They wished they could have found Mr. Rutter (registration secretary) more work in the way of enrolling new mem- bers. They s'neerely -hoped that shortly the numbers would be so large that they would have to find him an assistant to enroll them more quickly Their total membership at the close of February was over four hundred, and they had an average attendance of about 260. Mention was also made of those who had passed away during iho year,and words of sympathy were uttered to the bereaved ones. To all their brethren who had had a bard straggle during the year— times of great depression, disappointments, shortness of work or family trouble—they extended their hand of sympathy with a strong hope and a loving prayer for I otter times and bright and happier eay:s. Their prize scheme worked well and the choice of books was good. The thirty-two books some of them had received during the sixteen years of their attendance were a reminder of the happy hours they bad spent at their beloved spiriteal home. During the past year 510 books had been obtained as prizes. The total of books distributed since the forma- tion of the society was 13,108 or an average of 819 per year. They were glad to have their President among them, and they hoped for him, and many happy days in their midst. He trusted their two vice-presidents would have the loyal support of the members. Con- cluding he hoped that they would all work for the good of their fellows raid the glory of God. The balance sheet was submitted by Mr. R. Atkinson, Mr. R. Mills reported on the visitation work, Mr. Joseph Owen the benevolent work, Mr. 11. Farrar, the Sick Club Owen, sheet, and Mr. W. Cresskill, the- Saving's Lank account. The following officers were • elected,: —Rev, John Yonge, president • Mr. B. Farrar and Mr. IL Atkinson, vice-presidents ; Mr. B. Farrar, secretary ; Mr. \V. Farrar, assistant secretary; Mr. R. Atkinson, treasurer ; Mr. W. Rutter, registration secretary ; Mr. R. Mills, absentee secretary ; Mr. W. Farrar and H. Farrar, markers ' • Messrs. T. Wood, H. Taker, D. McEachera, E. Smith, H. Osborne and C. 11. Mills, stewards- Mr. Joseph Owen, benevolent secretary; Mr. J. H. Archer, sick club secretary ; Mr. 11. Farrar, sick club treasurer;

Mr. John Ratcliffe, savings club secretary; Mr. H. Crosskill, savings club assistant secretary; Mr. R. Atkinson, savings dub treasurer. General Committee, Rev. J. Yonge, Messrs. B. Farrar, R. Atkinson, W. Farrar, H. Fart-sr, NV. Rutter, R. Mills, J. Owen, H. Taker, T. Wood, E. Smith, W. H. Payton, G. Walling: ton, W. Beswick, G. Smiley, W. Crosskill, D. W. McEachern, J: Bailey, G. Colley, C. Cotton, J. H. Archer, J. Gresty,.J. Ratcliffe, S. Massey, J. A. Bradley, A. Littlewood, J. Cotton, J. J. Cooper, F. Dimelow, W. Roberts, C. H. Mills. Henry Osborne, A. Thomas, H. Blane, H. Massey, J. Himsworth, A. Healey, W. Wake- field, Richard Daniels.

"The Incarnation the Key to Nature and Human Life." This was the subject of a sermon by the Rev. Thomas Miller, at Bold-street Wesleyan Church on Sunday evening, his text being: "All things were made by Him, with Him was not anything made: That which bath been made was life in-Him. And the life was the light of men." (John I-3-4.) In the course of his sermon he said that the eternal word by Whom all things were created ; the Light which illumined ell.ruinds in all ages and races; the life which bad energised all upward movements in individual and universal experi- ence; was identified with Jesus of Nazareth, whose bhistory was given in the Creed. The entrance of the Eternal spirit into human affairs, and upon the plane Of history was mysterious, hut it was not incredible. Since they believed in God, who sustained all things, there was.nothing mere natural than that He should enter into communion with man. The question of the Virgin Birth agitated many men. Some had made the Incar- nation depend upon it. Whilst believing in the Virgin Birth, he failed to see that it was absolutely essential to the Incarnation. That extraordinary means would be used for an extraordinary revelation was probable, but it was conceivable -that the ordinary process of Nature could not be used. The central feature was that the eternal spirit entered human nature in order to manifest itself more clearly than was possible through creatiou or providence. Jesus was nqt an evolu- tion, but an incarnation. He was a divine spirit, who entered into the tabernaclo_of the flesh, it was not the creation of mere physical forces or moral spiritual environment. Tee incarnation gave light upon the methods and nature of creation. Creation was the expre=sion of love. • Look at the harmony of nature, the source of its order, and the element of beauty. We would see it. The immanence of deity was only another phrase to express the universal presence of the spirit of Christ. Matter could only be adequately when inter- preted through Jesus. He was the revelation of the 'Father and also of the world. When the mysteries of this divine and human nature were understood, the mystery of the world would disappear. Concluding, he said that in the belief of Jesus they found inspiration for social tasks, and brotherhood was made a reality at this time of great awakenings in the East as well as in oiir beloved land, the people were counting for more than monarchs; democratic movements, and literary and scientific progre,s were but the manifestation of the divine lifeas Built in the intellect and heart. Especially as they confronted the great problem of sin. as sfen in the masses in our slums, let them never lose heart. Every heart was purer for Christ's coming. Human nature had been sacrificed by the Incarnation, arid ultimately everyone should yield to the higher life within the soul and all things sh,uld be SaLdued unto the Son.

Mr. Regiwild Potts, Clerk of the Peace for Cheshire, in a circular letter stated that the Standing Joint Com- mittee were of opinion that it is incumbent upon Boards of Guardians to provide for the reception of children (14 to 16 years) taken to a Workhouse by the Police anthorities, while under remand or waiting trial and asking if the Runcorn Guardians would agree to their workhouse being registered for 'such a purpose and upon that terms. If the Board of Guardians in the County did not co-operate with the Starling Joint Committee by assenting to this course it might be pesessary to provide.and establish separate institutions and the Committee were anxious to avoid the consider- able expenditure this would involve. Mr. Linaker : Have we any accommoslation ? Clerk : Yes. •

Woodwaid : I more that a copy of the resolu- tions be obtained and supplied to each member of the Board, and also that it may receive the earliest con- sideration of the Parliamentary Committee. Mr. Hallows seconded and the matter was carried. POOR LAW CONFBRENCE.

CENTRAL POOR LAW CONFERENCES.

The Clerk read a report of the delegates (Messrs. J. H. Davies, Charles Horton and himself) to the Central Poor Law Conference. The delegate& were strongly of opinion that it would he in the interests the good administration if the Board would appoint a committee to consider the finding and recommendations of the Royal ComnAssion of the Poor Law with a view to considering in which respects the rcetbods adopted in Runcorn thereon were deficient nnd by what means such deficiences could be hest remedied.

Mr. Davies, being one of the delegates of the con- ference said he should like to say a few words upon ft. Dr. Marnamara in his opening speech laid great stress upon the training of young children in the administra- tion of the Poor Law. In the discussion that followed it was stated that friendly societies should be encouraged and it was proved that owing to these societies a great amount of expenditure had he saved. Discufsion vias brought up with relation to the training of girls in the school and gnat stress was laid upon.the advisability of domestic economy being taught in preference to so much needlework. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Horton also added a few words. Mr. Davies said that from some of the discussion at the conference it made him feel proud that he belonged to the Runcorn Board of Guardians. (Hear, hear.) The reports were adopted, and the delegates were thanked for their services.

FISCAL DEBATE.

WOULD TARIFF REFORM BENEFIT THE IRON AND STEEL WORKERS?

Councillor J. Broadhurst and Mr. J.

Elwell Argue the Question.

The Thewlis-street Schoolroom was filled to its utmost capacity on Monday evening when the much- talked-of fiscal debate between Councillor J. Broad- hurst (Conservative Labour Party), and Mr. J. Elwell, junior (Progressive Labour League) took place. The topic was "t"Will Tariff Reform be to the advantage of the iron and steel workers of this country ?" and the audience was largely composed of representatives of this branch of industry who followed the discussion with keen interest and applauded when any points were made. When the protagonists with the chairman (Mr. C. Plinston, Speaker of the Local Parliament) took their places on the platform they were greeted with an encouraging. cheer. Councillor Broadhurst had a formidable array of books and documents con- veniently arranged on the platform, while Mr. Elwell produced a large portmanteau, the deep recesses of which were closely packed with Blue Books,statistical tables and other light literature. Amongst those present in the audience were Mr. C. E. Parker and Alderman Smethurst.

===== 0002601_19090515_art0067.txt | included=true | included =====
CHAIRMAN'S SPEECH.

The CHAIRMAN said the question of tariff reform uas pressing itself to the front mole and more every week and month that we lived. We had been passing through very severe trade depression and the workers ,of the country were feeling it very keenly. The question of unemployment was one that was bound to be taken up by whatever party might hold the reins of office. The Government of this land years ago was entirely in the hands of the upper classes, who did their best. Eventually it resolved itself into tbe hands of the middle clas es, and they alto endeavoured to do their best to govern the country rightly and justly. Those days, however, had passed, and undoubtedly the future of this country and of our great Empire rested in the hands of the working classes, and it was for those who were represen ativss of the working classes, net only in Warrington but in Lancashire and England, seriously to consider the question. They 'must not be biassed either on the .Liberal Free Trade side or the Conservative Tariff Reform side, but listen calmly, following all the arguments that were brought forward by both. sides, and weighing them seriously, because upon the proper solution of the question rested the welfare of our country. (Applause).

===== 0002601_19090515_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
COUNCILLOR BROADHURST OPENS THE DEBATE.

Councillor BROADTfURST, in opening the debate in the affirmative, said it was one of the proudest moments of his life to have the opportunity of standing there to defencl the rights of the British puddler and the British iron worker—that he should make the iron and steel that was required in this country and that the monies of the employer of labour should not be spent with the German or the foreigner. Were

at the present time kat is file to ry ? According to the General Federation of Trade Unions, which issued a manifesto a short time ago, 1,500,000 people were unemployed in th's country. The late Sir Henry Campbell Bannerman had made it clear to the whole world that there *era over 12,000,000 on the verge of starvation. With regard to tha iron and steel trade, at Barrow, the centre of that industry, they found anit 796 of the finest iron and steel workers in the world had been driven by necessity to go into the poor hews to labour at breaking stones for Is. Gd. per day, to maintain their starving wives and little children. At the same time on the river Lurie they found four large ships with thou- sans of tons of finished material that was going to be dumped down into this country for on' of the railway lines: To come nearer home, he had walked through the streets of.that ward every day. He had been invited into the homes of those iron and steel workers whom he had the honour to represent in the Council Chamber,and people had apologized to him because they had had to pawn pictures and even chairs to obtain fo,ld. Having dwelt upoi one particular case and contended that the conditions Were so bad that the man was driven either to break up his home, seek Poor Law relief and dis- ftanchise himself, or to steal and be sent to prison, Couucillor Broadhurst said he wished his hearers, in the light of such conditions, when they saw hundre3s of tons of steel being brought into the very works where these men should be making it, to ask themselves whether it was a bleSsing or whether they ought not rather to rise and say that this cursed law ought to be abolished for the benefit of the British iron and steel worker. Last year 203,937,472 bottles were imported into this country What did that mean to the iron and steel workers? It reguired hundreds of tons of paddled bars to make finished iron and hundreds of tons of finished iron to make the tubes which were needed at the glass works to blow the bottles. Ce,uncillor Broadhurst referred to the closing of local wire and chemical works and to decreased trade with the chief protected nations on the part of a certain firm of wire manufacturers as other cquses of stagnation. Monks Hall and Co.'s works to remember that thousands of tons of cotton tyres were once made in the 8 inch mill. One of the leaning officials of the firm told him that when the McKinley tariff came into force it immediately killed that branch of their trade and they did none to-day. What had tariff reform done for the workers of those countries which had adopted it? Mr. McKinley's tariff imposed a very heavy duty upon all iron and steel goods going into America,, Mr. McKinley behig of opinion that the American iron and steel workera should make what was required. In the year 1891 we exported 335,000 tons of tin plates to that country but, according to Mr. James Cox (secretary of the Iron and Steel Workers) the imposition of the tariff had inflicted such a loss upon our trade that it. had now dwindled to 60,000 tons per year. Mr. Cox, on the occasion of his visit to America. stated that their tin, plate: production had increased pro rata with the decrease of our exports to that country. At this stage the bell rang to signify the expiration of the allotted twenty minutes, and Councillor Broadhurst resumed his seat. 11IR. ELWELL REPLIES.

Mr. ELWELL said he considered it an honour to stand on that platform in defence of Free Trade. Councillor Broadhurst bad drawn a very dismal picture of the condition of things in this country ; otherwise he had given them a good speech and as good a defence of tariff reform or Protection as most men would be able to give. Hew far his deductions were workable remained to to seen. They had first to consider what were the circnmstances which brought the question so prominently before the country of late years. Mr. Chamberlain, only recently come from a visit to South Africa, where he had seen the devastation caused by the war, knowing that many pertinent ques- tions were being asked by the thinking people of this country, and being desirous of diverting their attention to some other topic, embarked on his Protectionist crusade without, in his (Mr. Elwell's) humble opinion, having given any careful and deliberate thought that was necessary to a subject of such magnitude. Had he done so, he would not have made

TILE MISTAKES AND GROSS BLUNDERS that he did at Glasgow, Greenock and other places. At Greenock he told us "Sugar is gone, silk is gone, iron is threatened." He quoted the report of an interview between an American journalist, and a director of the American Steel Trust. The journalist asked what the Steel Trust was going to do in view of the falling off of hone orders. 'Oh" laid the director, "We have

made all our preparations. We are not going to reduce our output ; we are not going to blow tut a single furnace. Ivo, if we did, that would be injurious to America; we should have to turn out of our works into the streets hundreds of thousands of American work- men, and therefore what we are going to do is to invade foreign markets." 'Accepting this

AMERICAS BUNKUM at its full value, Mr. Chamberlain commented as follows : "Hundreds of thous;inds of English work- men will be thrown out of employment in order to make room for the same number of American workmen who are kept in employment during bad times by this system." What had been the result ? Mr. Chamber- lain's statement was made on October lth, 1903. The Keystone Furnace iii Pennsylvania was blown out on October 14th and this blowing out of furnaces in the United States continued throughout November and December, men being dismissed by thousands at a time. Councillor Broadhurst had drawn a dismal picture of the iron and steel workers of this country, but he (Mr. Elwell) said it was nothing to compare with what existed in America at that time and during the past ye (Applause). He admitted that our trade had ben passing through a severe crisis during the past year, but what had been the condition of things in American? Our trade went down, but American trade went down more than ten times as much. (Applause). Dien Mr. Chamberlain appealed in the course of his missionary tour to the Blue Book then in course of compilation as the one thing necessary to convince everyone that Protection must he adopted .by ths country. Unfortunately for Mr. Chamberlain, what he had proclaimed and looked forward to to prove his assertions that the trade of this country was as goo 3 RS gone, what he looked forward to bless his cause, turned to curse it aryl bler:s the Free Trade policy adopted by this country over fifty years ego—(applause)—byproving that under Free Trade the conditions of the people had improved more than those of Protectionist countries, that the irnportand export trade of this small country was greater than that of America with its vast natura, resources and over 80 million population, or Germaty or any other country on the face of the globe. So once more Mr.. Chamberlain was disappoiiited in his expected allies, and in his disappointment he arrogated to himself the power and authority of the Government of this country. He siid in effect if not in words "I will call a Commission, I will appoint my own judges and call my own wit nesses-; their names will be snepressed so that no one outside may know who they are, awl the outcome of the deliberations shall be given to the people of this country to prove that Protection must he adopted." No workers,

NO REPRESENTATIVES OF THE WORKERS or consumers, were beard by the Commission. Were they to have no voice in the disposal of their labour, or the price to be paid for the articles they required to consume and use? Free Traders raid that if a Commission were necessary why not let it be a National Commission, appointed by the Government to call witnesses from various sections of the community ? Then there would have been some probability of arriv- ing at a fair judgment. Yet., even here, with all the tariff reformers' endeavours to obtain a verdict, what did they find? They were ull at sixes and sevens. Some said a tax was not wanted, that its effect would undoubtedly be injurious to tbe. trade and welfare of the country, some advocated a 2} per cent, others 10 per cent. some 20 per cent. some 25 per cent., and yet again others who_ would tax up t..) 130 per cent, and furthermore our Colonies must adopt such a tariff, and give us such a prefei ence that they must have at least 10 per cont. advantage over the foreigner in the Colonial market. Were our Colonies prepared to do this ? Sir Wilfrid Laurier said he would nither see the disruption of the Empire than that Canlda should sacrifice her fiscal independence. (Applause). Where. was the evidence that the iron and steel trAde of this country was on the decline? In 1850 our

EXPORTS OF IRON AND STEEL AND METAL amounted to 767,734 tons. value £8,785,000; 1860, 1,438.000 tons, value £17,000,009; 1870, 2,500,000 tons: value £28,000,000 ; 1880, 3,750,000 tons, value £28,390,000: 1890, 4,001,000 tons, value £31,500,000; 1900, 3,416,752 tons, value £31,623,353; 1905, 3,721,382 tons, value £31,826,000; 1907, 5,152,227 tons, vslue £46,500,000; 1908, 4,100,976 tons, value £36,500,000. The condition of things immediately following 1900 was the outcome of the war in South Africa. The great demand for coal for our steamships to convey troops there forced the price of coal up, and there were no materials in the manufacture of which coal entered 8.3 largely as iron and steel. It was a well-known fact that the pig iron and semi-manufactured steel which came into this country in 1902—pig iron largely and most cheaply from Canada—was a great advantage to many manufacturers of finished iron and steel, enabling them to retain much of the export trade they would have lost if they had been entirely depcndent upon home supplies, as evidenced by the Special Blue Book published on this subject in 1903 by the Conaervative Government. Referring to puddling, Mr. • Elwell pointed out that iron was gradually giving place to steel. This was one of the inevitable laws of progress. It was not possible to have progress on the one hand without decay on the other. As the old stage coach had to give way to railways, so iron had to give way to steel and no bolstering up by tariffs could bring again the state of things that existed in the iron trade years t'yro. Steel had come to stay, whether we wished it or no, and ve most _ adapt ourselves to the altered condition of the Made. In ,proof of there changing conditions, he would rejer them to the White Paper issued quite recently to the order of the House of Commons, wherein it was stated that fully five-sixths of the pig iron produced in the world was utilized in the production of steel :-63 per cent, of that produced in the Uuited Kingdom ' • 92 per cent, of that produced in Germany; 90 per cent, of that produced in the United States. Mr. Elwell contended that both in regard to production, number employed, wages and profltS, the trade hal made satisfactory progress in recent years, and there was no justification for the hopeless wail of despondency from the Protectionists. Mr. Cox attributed the success of the American steel trade very largely to the vast natural resources, cheap transit &c. which the country possessed. No doubt it was helped

by tariffs at the outset, but it would have been viol! more healthy and natural if it had grown up withou'' tat in.

piIIIARRINGTON CHAPTERS. THOMAS LETHERBROW- A WARRINGTON WORTHY.

There are many Warringtonians who deserve from their native town a word of grateful remembrance. Apart from the famous and fortunate there have been many whose lives, by high character and useful- ness reflect credit upon the town of their nativity. Such was Thomas Letherbrow whose description of the Warrington of his youth we printed last week. His work was doneln Manchester and his name is honoured there in literary and artistic circles, but Warrington was the place of his youth, of his inspiration, and, to the end of life, of his most cherished memories.

His mother was Sarah Phillips, the hand- some, joyous, clever daughter of Peter and I Hannah Phillips, and he was born in 1825. The family migrated to Manchester and he ; was indentured to Samuel Brooks of the well- known Brooks Bank. For sixty years he served the firm and climbed to a high posi- tion. As a young man lit) knew William Brooks, the founder of the 'bank, and he lived to see the fifth generation after him. In illustration of the difference time has made he tells that Samuel Brooks, the principal of the bank used regularly to walk to the butcher's shop and carry back the beef for his dinner, and used in the evenings to call on the parents of the young men int his employ to report upon their progress. If we place in contrast to this the relation- ship between a mpdern millionaire employer and his employees, we realize the vast changes that have crept over our commercial life. Before Thomas Letherbrow left War- rington he had received a strong impetus towards art from his maternal uncle, and he remained a devoted disciple of both art and literature to the end of life. By the kind- ness of his widow two of his pictures have been presented to the Warrington Art Gallery, and will serve, we hope, as per- manent reminders of his association with the town.

But some brief snatches from his autobiography will be much more interesting than any sketch we can write. We choose a passage concerning his grandfather, Peter Phillips.

===== 0002601_19090515_art0077.txt | included=true | included =====
PETER PHILLIPS.

"Sitting here in enforced idleness which has already lasted many weeks my thoughts have often gone back to the scenes of my childhood. They have been forcibly tarned in that direction by the reading of a letter in ishe Manchester "City News." The writer had conversed with a man who had assisted in the construction of the Manchester and Liverpool railway more than sixty years ago, who, he seemed to think, was the only sur- vivor of that band of workers who were the pioneers of the railway system. Yet curiously enough, the harkl that traces these lines,then a very small one, helped to shape some of its accessories. It was in my grandfather's workshop .at Warrington, and while I sit musing here the whole scene comes back to me, with its picturesque interior, and the faces and voices long since swallowed up in the grave. As for good Peter Phillips him- self, there is Ikardly anyone in the old town who remembeis him, though the denomina- tion of which he was a pioneer honours him, and the Connexional Almanac records that he was six times its president and keeps his portrait with that of his wife Hannah well in the eye and mind of its readArs. "My maternal grandfather, Peter Phillips, preacher and cabinet-maker, was a man of marked originality. According to his views of religion, a man should, like St. Paul, work for his bread during the day and preach the Gospel without fee. Acting on these prin- ciples he never "got on " in a worldly point of view, while his brother John rose to high esteem as a paid minister and even counted a, provincial Mayor among his offspring. A portrait of my grandfather, made by me, is still extant. He was a sturdy man of patri- archal aspect, serious of speech, grave-eyed. and wore a Suit ofQuaker's garb, an ample skirted coat, white kerchief, knee-breeches, , and blue knitted stockings with low shoes. He was widely known as the pioneer of the Quaker Methodists (now the Independent Methodists) and laboured hard every Sabbath and, in spare hours, among the weavers and the colliers and in the rural district that lay for twenty . miles round Warrington. He walked many thousands of miles in the course of his long life, and was higkly esteemed and sought out in his humble dwelling by such men as Adam Clark, Lorenzo Dow and Wilson Mackay. - -

"He had bunt for himiself a workshop not far from the Red Lion, and it was my delight to go up into the loft and watch the various processes going on. I remember well the picturesque interior, the shavings and sawdust on the floors and the pulleys in the roof connected with a big wheel down below. Three of his six sons worked along with him. Master, sons, and workmen were all pretty much on an equality, and there were plenty of jokes, fragments of songs,and what Pepys calls " good discourse " when the master deemed it best to give things a serious turn. But no oaths were allowed, though sometimes party feeling ran high and Whig and Tory fell foul of each other. "In the big grate burned a fire of shavings and log ends. Occasionally, to my great joy a sort of iron gibbet was let down the big chimney and displayed a row of hams, juicy and shining, and of a rich sienna brown, which had been hung there to be flavoured by the aromatic pine and oak shavings. Never were such collops as these hams yielded

===== 0002601_19090515_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW I HELPED STEPHENSON.

"Many were my visits to the workshops and it was on one of these occasions that I actually took part, in my small way, in shaping some of the accessories of the famous railway of Stephenson. "My grandfather,his proper line of business being slack, took a contract to furnish a certain number of wooden balls or plugs with iron spikes in them, which were to be used in the construction of the iron road. They were made by means of a steel punch fixed firmly in a wooden handle of the head. This punch was placed on a piece of wood, and the head was struck smartly with a wooden mallet and a cylindrical peg was produced. Into this was fixed an iron spike, and it was then ready for use. I persuade.d my uncles to let me try this operation, and to my great glee found that I could produce those wooden pegs with considerable success. And so I may say with truth that I assisted in the construction of the Liverpool and Manchester line, the first passenger railway in the world.

===== 0002601_19090515_art0079.txt | included=true | included =====
A TENDER LAMENT.

"When I was last in the town of my birth it was to lay in the grave the last of the friends of my youthful days left on the old spot, and it was difficult to believe that it was the same heaven and earth that rejoiced my young soul. We are told that the revolution brought about by-the invention of the loco- motive is a, beneaeient one, It may be so,

but I look in vain for that life of plain living and high thinking of which my grandwrents were an example. The sky is hicillen in smoke; the bright river of my youth is loaded with filth from the teeming cities of industry ; and the friendly faces and familiar voices—they too are gone! As Walter Vonder Vogelweide sings: My once merry comrades aged are and slow,

The old fields are empty, the—old woods laid low, Only the wide water flows as it did flow. "But the old place is peopled again as I close my eyes and dream. I see my most- loved uncle, Joe Phillips, and hear his sweet voice. We are 6n the river and he is con- trolling the boat. I think I can still hear his fine tenor voice ringing out over the water as we row up with the flood tide from Fiddlers' Ferry, Halton Castle dark against the sky and the summer moon rising over the woods. One plays the cornet and our voices join in the Canadian boat song. "Of that happy boat crew one rests in the Cemetery of Constantinople, another found a grave in Sydney, two more perished in the bush and no man knows their resting place. "It seems but the other day that I left the clean, pleasant old town by the Mersey and stepped on board the Old Quay Packet a wondering, heedless lad. I was clutching in my hand my most precious possession, a toy man of war'made for me by Captain Leigh. They all followed me, my grandfather, grandmother, my pretty black-eyed aunts and the uncles who were at home, and many were the blessings, tears and kisses bestowed on me.

" And it is sixty-five years ago, but the memory of a happy childhood shines forth undimmed by lapse of years. and leaves a bright track of memory that will last with life."

A. M.

===== 0002601_19090515_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
OUR MISCELLANY.

By the courtesy of Mr. Robert Lewis, of the Old Fox, we have been able to scan an old placard posted fifty years ago on the eye of a Municipal election. Old Warrington neVer lacked wags and jesters and the town was often set "on a roar" by clever ouffoonery.Much of the.coarse fun is better forgotten, but we must not judge the fun-makers too harshly. AS a cultured old Warringtonian reminded us in the notes quoted last week, the town had. few diversions and the man who could raise a laugh was a public benefactor. At election times the fun came freely and the public nomination or hustings gave everyone who had the least wit a glorious opportunity. Somebody who has a memory long enough could write a capital 'Chapter or two on the diversions of fifty years ago.

* * :*

Fifty years ago last October the town was freely plavardecl by a poster issued in antici- pation of the Municipal Elections, and the sheet is a monument of wit and buffoonery. It announces: A CORRECT LIST OF THE WARRINGTON MUNICIPAL RACES. _

These Notorious Races will be revived on November Ist, 1858.

As 'Stewa4s T. G. Rylands, Henry White and B. Piefroint, Esqs., are named, Shaw Thewlis, Esq., is described as Judge, and C. N. Spinks, Esq., as Clerk of the Course and Starter. The prize in the first race is A Model (in brass) of the Market Fountain" open " for asses of all ages." Another prize is "The Scramble Wharf Plate of £l4l for asses. to be ridden by members of the Cor- poration; Twice round the Market Hall to Warrington Bridge and back." Among the eight entries is the name of Peter Rylands, whose distinguishing marks are given as "tiger face and rainbow stripes." In another race for the "Borough Cup" Peter's colour is chameleon.

** * *

There wore debates about a Buttetmarket- street urinal in those days, for one of the prizes announced is "A model of the Urinal in Buttermarket-street," presented by those. who reside in the neighbourhood. For this race five councillors aro said to have entered.

** * *

The innkeepers of the town are announced as having shown their interests by present- ing a splendid bust of Alexander Mackie,Esq. They are said to have done this out of gratitude to him for attracting all their roughest customers to his wonderful Sunday evening lectures. In the list of entries is Philip P. Carpenter's Water Rat." We cannot interpret the moult municipal allu- sions which lurk in every line of this crowded poster, but we imagine that an old inhabitant would find it very entertaining. Echoes 'of. controversies and public jealousies--real or supposed—are evident, as in the announce- ment that one of the prizes will be" one of daggers which Hardy looked, when Chrimes put on the Civic robes."

** * * Sgme of the items we dare not transcribe. Skins were surely thicker in days when a poster like this could be t üblicly displayed. Such a sheet issued now would give ground for libel actions big enough to occupy his Majesty's judges for many a day.

SCRATCHED UNTIL HE BLED.

Bleeding Piles almost sent him mad,

till Zam-Buk Cured Him.

For two :ears Mr. Reuben Betts, a miner, of 21, Wharfe-road., Pinxton, near Alfreton,l,DerbY, experi- enced the horrors of bleeding piles. Then he followed the advice of a work-mate and tried Zam-Buk, with the result that he was quickly and miraculously freed from the torturing disease. Telling his story to an "Alfreton Advertiser" reporter, Mr. Betts said :—" Two years ago I began to suffer from piles, and the pain from the 'Constant burning itch was at times maddening. The doctor eve me an injection and special ointment and medi- cine, but the piles never got a bit easier. During the. daytime the pain was almost unbearable, but at night as soon as I got warm in bed the itching leas some- thing terrible. I was obliged to scratch to try to get a little ease. and I usually 6crached unti I bled freely. When I sat down or walk ed-about I suffered agonies from the tormenting pain and itch, while the constant bleeding made me so weak I had to give up work. "I tried many so-called remedies, but as month after month went by I lost all hope. Really, when a mate urged me to try Zatnlßuk, I first felt it was useless to try anything more, but just to please him I bought a box and used some the same night. Almost immediately after I apPlied. Zam-Buk I was able to get some sound sleep, for the first time for many months. When I awoke the pain was not nearly s 6 acute, and a 'further liberal dressingwith this grand balm gave me ease during the day. "I repeated the Zam-Buk dressings regularly and I made wonderful progress towards a complete cure. My friends were astonished to see Me going to my work cheerfully again, and I was not slow to tell them what was curing me. Well, the itching and burning died away -altogether as I continued with Zam-Buk., and the piles were gradually overcome, and soon I was quite free. That was some months Itgo, aad there has not been the slightest sign of a return of this terrible complaint since. I shall never forget the debt I owe to Zam-Buk for my wonderful cure." . THE ROSTHERNE BAN.

CYCLIST INVASION AND PROTEST MEETING.

Quiet little Rostherne was not itself on Saturday. _ That poetic peace and tranquillity Which usually broods Over the picturesque _village was broken by the invasion of a great army of cyclists—drawn there in protest against Lord Egerton's edict forbidding his tenants to provide visitors with refreshments. had Lord Egerton wished the spot to be over-run with pleasure makers be could not have adopted a speedier or more effective means of attaining that object. As a rule, the town-dwellers enjoying Rostherne's rural delights may be numbered under the hundred at any one time, except on some special holiday, but on Saturday they came in their thousands until it was hard work to move along the. old-world village lane. The lord of the manor's arbitrary action has aroused feelings of deep indignation amongst the respectable, • well-conducted people,who comprise the vast majority of Rostherne's visitors, .and they bitterly resent any imputation that unseemly behaviour on their part has necessitated such an extreme prohibition. On Saturday all roads led to Rostherne. The spacial objective of the cyclists was a great protest meeting and probably never before has such a deluge of oratory bead let ! loose on the village. It was a memorable day, to be for ever written large in local annals, and the inhabi- tants looked on in wonderment that such things could happen within the shadow of the Manor.

Eien the weather lent its best countenance to the dembnstration. 'Sunshine of midsummer brilliance, blue sky flecked with the lightest of clondlets, and. a not unpleasant breeze—such were the ideal condi- tions. The country side was presented in its most alluring aspect. Cycling from Warrington rig Lymm, one saw hundreds of fruit trees covered With rich blossom. The hedges had burst into full leaf and gardens Were bright with fresh spring flowers. As we spull'along secluded by-ways, 'where motors trouble not, it was difficult ,to conjure up visions of the mighty host which we had been led to expect would encompass Lord Egerton's demesne. But on reach- ing Bucklow Hill, all was changed. From every direction cyclists were streaming up, and mine host of" The Swan " a roaring business, for this-was the last hous':.,, of call before entering the forbidden

THE CROWD ENTERINC ROSTHERNE ON SATURDAY.

land. One wheeler who had already been to Rostherne but had evidently found it too full for his liking, was heard to declare " There's millions of them down there." A slight eiaggeration—but it Showed that he had been impressed. The road from Bucklow Hill to Rostheino. is one of the most beautiful mile lengths to be found in Cheshire. It runs through a kind of glen, arched with spreading trees, and, in the summer, edged with a wealth of bracken, and the whole effect is one of sylvan charm. Entering Rostherne, we found the place already in possessimt of the wheelers—and more were arriving, every minute. Down the hill and past the square- towered church they came in an irresistible phalanx. &any of the machines were adorned with rod and green ribbons and one bore the device " We demand the right to tea "-7-which neatly hit off the sentiment of the moment. As a buttonhole a large number Of the cyClists displayed a Small card with the words " Cyclists' protest." How 'many protesters gathered in Rostherne in the course of the afternoon it would be impossible to say, but 8,000 would not be an 'excessive estimate. Everywhere one looked tilde were cyclists, dusty and cheerful. The majority of them had come from the Manchester district but there was a fair sprinkling of Warrington faces. . One local cycling club—Sankey .Bridges Wesleyan—was at Rostherne in fulfilment of a. fixture arranged some time ago, but the peculiar circumstances lent a special piquancy to the outing. As showing how the fame of the village and its lord has spread, the writer . was in conversation with a rider who had travelled from Littleborough, near Road*, to see for himself the beauties of a resort which has been so much described of late. The villagers were at first inclined to view the invasion with misgivings and cast furtive - glanees at the procession from behind the curtains, but when -they saw ,that nothing more dangerous than peaceful persuasion was contemplated, they came to their doors and seemed to enjoy time novelty of the affair. One ancient dame was heard to ask a visitor quite Proudly: "nave you polite to see our parade ?" in view of the edict many of the cyclists had brought theii:victuals with them and were to be seen discus Sing sandwiches and other dainties by the road side. The village Post Office was besiged with thirsty souls who bought lemonade, "Orangeade " and other drinks With tempt- ing names, as rapidly as the perspiring assistants -behind the counter could serve them out. At only one place—the farm of Mrs. -Johnson, who has been called the village Hampden—were teas to be had,and the trade was such that the proprietress, who has decided to defy the ban, must have blessedthe happy inspiration which prompted the great protest gathering.

Most of the crowd seemed to be drawn as by magnetic influence to the cricket field, and on entering it was discovered that the much-talked-of meeting' was in progress. There is a. notice to the effect that " no vehicles, including cyclists, are permitted in the field," but every one tool French leave, and a curious scene was presented. Some cyclists, overcome with their exertions,were reclining in luxurious ease on the grass, and machines were stacked against the hedge or on.the field itself, about a dozen deep. Other less trustful visitors • stuck to their " bikes " as if. they were the most precious of possessions, and stood patiently to listen to the speeches. Hard by a cricket Match was in progress,. but there .did not seem to be many bard •hitters,--or the ball might easily have landed in the midst of the meeting ! Picturesque indeed is the outlook from the field. In the back- ground stands the Church, with its quiet and beauti- fully kept graveyard, and its brown towers from which the musical chimes break the stillness of the air, while to the fore ,that fine sheet of water, the mere, sparkles as ajewel in the sunlight. With the meeting itself we were frankly dis- appointed. It was a Socialist meeting pure and simple, -organized by the Manchester Clarion Scouts , who will no doubt be pluming themselves on their astuteness in seizing this opportunity for sewing the seeds of Socialism. The protest would, however, have gained in weight had it been non-political—a cyclists' meeting to object. to 'a ban chiefly affecting cyclists. The vast majority, of the visitors did not go to Rostherne to listen to diatribes against landlordism and.capitalism in general, and arguments for land nationalization in particular, but to record a calm but firm protest against what they cbnceived to be an interference with their just rights. Some of the wild and irrelevant things that were said did not commend themselyes, and. many cyclists took their departure

their way homewards and the village to regain some of its normal calm. When, in the cool of the evening, we set out for smoky Warrington, the coach and pair of the Egerton's passed along the village lane. We Were told that it was going to Astley station to meet his lordship who had been away from his charming manor for the day. It was a pity he chose to turn his back on the demonstration, for had he stayed to see the influx of cyclists from near and far he would pro- bably have been convinced as to the genuineness and sincerity of the opposition to the ungenerous course ' which he has thought fit to pacsue. Orderliness was the keynote of the proceedings from beginning to end. The conduct of the protesters was exemplary and the village police force—reinforced for the occasion to the strength of six - had the pleasantest task imaginable. They mopped their brows and looked on placidly, though it must have seemed a rather cruel home thrust when one of the speakers declared; "The police have the power to turn us off this field, but I think we would be too many for them ! " " charge " would certainly have been futile under the circumstances, and Robeit. smiled tolerantly and allowed the orators to indulge themselves to their hearts' content.

Lord Egerton Firm.

On Monday a Manchester journalist interviewed Lord Egerton at Tatton Hall and elicited his views with reference to the protest, which his lcirdship said was a very orderly one," though," he added, " I have had to clean up since." " There was the ordinary reyalt of a big demon- stration such as you have in the parks of London, litter galore, but in those cases the parks belong to the people, whilst here they had no permission to go into the field where the demonstration was held."

Asked if he contemplated taking any 'action against the organisers, his Lordship replied. " Who is there to take action against? They have had their say, and I think nobody paid much attention to what they did s my. The people who suffered most were the visititg cricket teams who were vory much disturbed." " Will the meeting have any effect upon your Lordship? "

"Not the slightest. I have had ample support from respectable people, and plenty of them." A QUESTION IN THE COIINIONS.

Mr. Levy Lever, the Liberal member for tke Harwich division of Essex on Monday; in the House of Commons asked the President of the Local Govern- ment Board whether his" attention had been called to a notice issued by the proprietor to the villagers on his estate at Rostherne, Ashley, and Mobberley, Cheshire, forbidding any cottagers to receive any person whatsoever in their cottages for the purpose of the sale or consumption of any refreshment under penalty of instant notice to quit, and if he would con sider the advisabilily of inserting a clause in the Housing Bill to give cottagers some security of tenure. Mr. Burns, in a written reply, says : I have seen a newspaper statement containing a notice to the effect mentioned. I should .hope that the proprietor may see his way to relax the prohibition referred to, but I doubt whether .it would be practicable to insert in the Housing Bill a clause dealing with the difficult subject alluded to by my hon. friend.

PUIBERTON

CHAPTER XXX. (Continued)

[Jules rarman s'.nds to Henry Gastonard some account of his stewardship.]

It is true, Monsieur, that the crime itself gives no clue to such Ft study of intention. At the fiist blush I might argue that the disappearance of Madame, who, I do not doubt, has been forcibly abducted from your house, _points_ to thfact _that _blackmail_ is the issue; but we arc not to forget that an alternative presents itseif, and that these men having committed this crime, must for very safety's 'sake also silence the solitary witness of it. So they abduct Madame, and held her as a hostage either until they gain a place of safety or have purchased her silence in another way.

Of ,these alternatives, it is my sincere hope that the former approximates to the truth rather than to the latter. Men whose one desire is gain will resort to extreme measures reluctantly. I should fear lea from the avowed criminals of Paris, who have a precious secret to sell, than from others whose projects may be more daring. such evidence as. I can collect helps me to the belief that it is with the criminals of Paris that I have to deal.

Permit me to recapitulate this evidence as briefly ad may be.

And firstly, that of your neighbour, Cap- tain Esmond, the officer of Marine who wit- nessed the Count's arrival at your house. This he declares to have been within ten minutes of your departure—so proving that the Count was aware of your absence and de sired immediately to avail himself of an un- expected opportunity. Secondly, the evidence of the cabman Williams, who testifies that a sniall covered- motor-car waited the third part of an hour or more at the corner of the street near by the great house, Bell Moor, and passed him later on near Swiss Cottage station, going at a great pace in the direction of Regent's Park Thirdly, the evidence of the boy, Ham Carter, who- spoke to the driver of the car and obtained' an answer—he believes in the French tongue, but is unable to. say; so ignorant of any other tongue is he. Fourthly, the evidence of the servant girl, Cecily Rayner, who declares that she saw a man climbing over the garden wall of No. 4 about the hour of this crime, and that shr mentioned the matter to her mistress, who immediately went out to the garden but di, covered the house to be in darkness and hear( no sound of any kind.

This, Monsieur, is our evidence. To -me th, conclusions are very natural I—That the Count was murdered almos4. immediately he entered your -house. 2—That the assassin entered by way of thei garden, passed through your sitting-room and struck the unhappy roan while he wal actually in conversation with Mpdame.

3—That this crime was so- swift, so brutal, and so renforseless that your wife fell in a faint—and so tying was carried immediately to the carriage without being able to offer any resistance whatever. 4—That the assassin was either in the ser vice of the Count, or so situated as to be ir possesaion of his plans and intentions, 'fine thus able to forestall. them. _

Such, Monsieur; is the result of the work I have had the honour to do for you. I co, fess with regret that we have dug but a poor foundation, and that the corner-stone of our house is yet to be laid. This task must be ac- complished not in London ,but in .Paris. I leave to-night by the boat train from Charing Cross and beg yo tt to accompany me. For in Paris alone, --Monsieur, shall we dis- cover why the Count il'Antoine visited Madame Gastonard, and what was the secret, so precious to hint and to others, which he could disclose to her alone. I have the honour to be, Monsieur, Your obedient servant, • JULES lIENRY FARMAN.

Author of "The Iron Pirate " "The

Hundred Days," "A Puritan's Wife,"

etc,, etc,

Café des Assassins when we' vieite4 it the second time. If we risked much, you will believe how little any thought of personal danger deterred us.- Indeed, I do truly be- lieve that a ruffian there was within an ace of drawing a pistol upon us both--but Far• man is the master of such men as these, and I am never afraid in his company. I should tell you that it was mid-day when we visited the Cafe and found no one in charge but a very pretty and very ragged little French girl sitting before a charcoal stove in tho outer room. She knew Jules Farman well, and asked him -pointedly% why he came there a second time in as many days. When he answered her evasively she ran away to tell someone else, who proved to be a dirty and unwashed ruffian, by name Rogers —fore he was an Englishman fong known to the English police and well watched- by the French. This fellow 'was already drunk—a bottle of spirits stood by. the side of., his filthy bed; a revolver lay close to his hand. Had he been sober, there would have been civility enough; but in his mad state he flourished his pistol iqildly upon our entrance and would have shot us for a word. In the end Farman frightened him thoroughly, and "lie told us somewhat abjectly to follow the giant Jean-le-Mont and to put our question, to him.

_ It is possible that this is a clue; it may be mere coincidence. You will not hare for- gotten my letter to you in which I told you of the robbery at the Quat-Z-Arts ball, of Mimi's recovery of my gold cigarette-case from this very ruffian, and of his subsequent threats against her. But I find it hard to believe that a monster, whose one ambition of the day is to steal money for his drink to- anorrow should remember so pitiful an inci- 'dent,- much less the name of one of the thou- /sand friendlees girls who haunt the Butte and its vicinity.

In this Jule e Farman agrees with me—but he asks the pertinent question, Is it not poe. tsible that this fellow may be "the agent of 'others unknown, and that all he has done has 7-ysen done for money which conies to him from this undiscovered source? I hope that it may ibe so. The police of London are searching Soho and other haunts of Frenchmen for any news of him—the police here are not less 'diligent. You will not be angry with me, Paddy. for speaking of another matter. and one of -which you will hear with little pleasure Yesterday I had a letter from Lea d'Alen (_;on inviting me to her house, and assuring -aie that Monsieur Is Capitaine was heartily ashamed of his treatment of me. There has been, I understand, something resembling a reconciliation between the pair, though God knows how long a woman will be content to be the amiable companion of * man who prefers to go to bed at eleven o'clock at _night and considers a dinner at Armenenville as the first -instalment of his purgatory. I shall not go to her house, be sure—nor could I contemplate such an infamy as any renewal of this acquaintanceship would imply. None the less, I am troubled—for she speaks in a postscript of her ability to help me, and de- clures that my happiness may depend upon a prompt response to her invitation. You say that you arc in London awaiting my _return. I -can give you no definite news of this, but I could with it rather sooner hen later. Everything here reminds me of Mimi aed the happy days. I visited the old Maison du bon Tabac the other day and spent an hour in its empty rooms. Not •a scrawl upon ite shabby walls, not a broken pane in its win- dows but spoke to me of my little wife and •the golden days which are no more. And just as it is tumbling into decay, so, Paddy, is the house of my own life falling. I see the great city of Paris below me—it speaks of eternal things and of the darkness which is eternal. The green woods rise beyond, and I remember that they gave me Mimi in the days of the springtime, even as their falling leaves may hide her to-day from my sight. All that is here, the voice once musical of Paris, the glare of her lights, the rolling traffic in the streets, the unceasing business of pleasure—all this has no meaning for me. I pass by as a man who has no place in such a pageant, who must walk apart until the end. Even the memory, of the golden days has become an evil thing. I shut the old pic- tures from my eyes, but they rise up to mock me. Ah, Paddy, the day is dark in- deed, when a man's youth stands to him :for an evil memory, and he would blot the yester- day of life from .the book he has written. Write to me often, old friend. Remember how very much I am alone. If circumstances seem to promise a• continued stay here, I will beg you to come to me. Meanwhile, find me, as always, dear Paddy, yonr friend, HARRY GASTONARD.

CHAPTER XXXI.

[Henry Gastonard sends Paddy O'Connell seine account of his labours in Paris.) Hotel St. Paul, Paris. October 19th, 1905. Dear Paddy,—Your letter speaks of a good heart and a true 'friendship, In your own words, God bless you for it. I cannot tell you what I have suffered during these terrible days. There are few to whom I would speak of it. Sometimes I wish to God that I could wake no more. The world- moves about me as a rushing sea of which I am afraid. I fear for my very rea- son.

Consider it all and bear with me. 1 was the happiest man in Europe before this trouble came. Nothing in life- but Mimi mattered then. Oh, Paddy, if I could tell you what it was to have her always with me— to wake each day and find her head upon my pillow, to sleep with her white arms about my neck. None of us knew her a little bit in the old deys. But, Paddy, I learned to knew her; I learned the truth which few men learn—the secret was mine—the sweet- ness of it past belief. And to say she is gone from me! If she were dead, the bitterness of the truth could not be more poignant. I think of every city as her prison; I pass no house in Paris that I do not say, If Mimi were there! A thou- sand suppositions of life and death torment me every day. Does she know what I suffer? Is it not possible that she will send a mes- sage to me? Day anewers nothing—the nights are silent. I can butewait and pray. You say that you arrived in London upon the morning after my departure. Ido not ask you to come to me in Paris, because I know not whether Paris will be my home to- morrow, or some other city to which destiny may lead me. Be sure that I am prepared for anything. I work with Jules Farman literally from the rising of the sun until mid- night. We have visited more dens in Paris than I would have numbered for all the slums of France. And 'we know no more than the meanest servant of the police, who writes his theories in five folios, where my wife is hid- den, or what this stupendous mystery may be. You will have read my letter in the Eng- lish papers, and I have little to add to it to- day. It was my purpose to remove the coWardly suspicions which hover about the name of one of the purest of women, and thie I believe that I have done. Is it not monstrous to see how ready the world is to doubt a woman's honour, how willing to anticipate her guilt. No reason governs the tongue of scandal, nor does iustice .curb it. Here was a pretty •French girl—she is immoral, says slander. A man visits her—a man she has never seen before—he is her lover, says the multitude. He is foully murdered in her house—she must be the murderess. An Eng- lish police, never clever when any gift of subtlety is demanded, will accept no story but the one which ministers to its love of the commonplace. A French police, readier to look further afield, still believes that the Count was Mimi'e lover. And _ I am alone against these. My love can but speak in a voice which the clamour of convietion would .drown. Ah, Paddy, if I could but call these slanderers one by one before me; could com- pel them to answer me; could wring a cry of justice from their throats. For I alone knew what Mimi was, and alone I must de- fend her. I have. told you that we vieitcd many of the dens upon the further side of the Butte. Our reward is some story old the disappear- ance of the notorious ruffian. Jean-le-Mont. and of his aforetime accomplice Baiele-Due the apeCilee This wee Icamed at ti4Q cad

CHAPTER XXXII.

[ln which Paddy O'Connell advised his friend Harry to pay a visit.] Jaelc Straw's Castle, Hampstead. October 21st, 1905.

Dear Harry,—l have no telegram from you this morning, and am remaining here. Be good enough to wire upon receipt of this to say if you would sooner have me in Paris or London—for 'tis little I care where, so long as I may be of service to you. Your letter to the daily papers has done a power of good. I had no idea that any friend of mine could write with so much feeling and good sense, and I congratulate you upon it. The town' I ain told, continues to talk of little else—but you have given the affair a new turn, and the newspaper editors have more than they can do with the letters that come to them.

Now, my boy, 1 am going to speak very plainly to you. Had you asked me a month ago whether you should go to Madame Lea's house, I would have turned my back upon you for the question, and put it cut of your power to ask me another for many a long day. _ . . .

hut this is not my advice this morning. Tire is something lying at the back of my head which may, be common sense or may be a fool's burden; but it is crying to me all the time that a woman may be the heart of ttiis mystery, and to a woman you may well go for that news which no man is able to give you. I say, go to this Lea d'Alencon and hear what she has to tell you. When your dear wife is found, 'twill he Paddy O'Connell who will make his advice good and relieve. you of the burden of it. Go to her, and ask tier plainly what is the meaning of the postscript to her letter. She'll tell you in five minutes. There never vet was a •woman born who could keep good or bad news from the man who meant to have it from her. .

I shall say no more, lest I should put thoughts into your mind and inspire you with a hope that has no justification in the facts. One thing Ido ask you to believe, and it is this: that if your wife is alive and well, and I believe her to bo both, we shall have a message from her before Many days have run. 'Tis a poor sort of a house which can keep a clever woman from speaking out of its windows when she has the mind to be eloquent—and Mimi is no singing bird to be content with a spoonful of canary seed. We shall hear from her, I say, and the news will be good news. So go to visit Madame with a light heart—and be sure you carry Yourself well before her—for a woman tells Mile to a coward, and this lady may have much to tell. I would have you to know that your cousin Martha stands with me in this opinion, and is all for your going to Madame Lea. CHAPTER XXXIII. [The Reverend Arthur Warrington thanks Paddy O'Connell for services rendered.] The Red Farm, Beldon, Suffolk. Eve of the Feast of St. Raphael. Dear Mr. O'Conncll,—l am much obliged by your letter and ;packages containing the little books you were unfortunately unable to deliver to my poor cousin. I thank you also for your friendliness towards Mrs. War- rington during her mission of charity to the great Metropolis. That there is no further news concerning this unhappy affair distresses Me greatly. The wages of sin are dreadful indeed; lead- ing, it would appear, beyond the promises of Holy Writ to these awful mysteries this twentieth century brings before us." I have no doubt that poor Harry- meant well when he married this girl; but _ I cannot forget that he was not blessed by Holy, Church, and that he is now reaping the fruit of his indifference.

A home broken 'up, this dreadful suspicion hovering about his poor wife's name—oh, my clear sir, what moral lessons do not these things convey. Let us offer him what conso- lation we can, remembering with the poet Shakespeare that—murder, though it hive no tongue, will speak with most miraculous organ. • I pray God that these assassins will be brought to justice speedily.—And ant, with renewed thantke, my dear sir, yours faith- fully, . _ ARTHUR WARRINGTON.

Absolutely, no." Never met him while Toll were i.l Pltrifi?

"Never once—but remember my life. The Butte knows little of society—if there is any

democracy in this world, it is that of the atelier and the conservatoire. At the -Hotel St. Paul I was merely an English gentleman seeing Paris. Why should I moot the Count d'Antoine-"

"But your wife--" "Would you say that she knew him?" She paused and bit her lip. I could ima- gine that her thoughts were travelling again. From this moment., I cannot. tell you why, I began to suspect her. And, Paddy, remem- ber what suspicion meant to me, the hopes and fears of it, the straw upon the stream of a woman's caprice, the light,-upon the crest, to lose. which Were a torture.

"Would. you coy that Mimi knew the Count, Lca ?"

She smiled- now—a wan smile, not of jest, but of her own endeavour to deceive.

"There were few pretty womet whom the Count did not know-"

"Then you were among. the number?"

"la,met -him twice at. --the house of the Amencari, Madame Martin, and again at the Austrian Embassy. A very handsome man, one of those who bewitch women with ths notion that they have a thousand stories, but would never tell them. Oh, yes, I knew the Count."

"And you bc.sliove it possible that Mimi knew him?"

"Everything ig possible in Paris—did the not frequent the gardens?" "It is a lie—she knows the West as a tourist from my own country. Her, home was on the mountain."

"You say se, Henry—oh, forgive me, I am trying to help you. If she did not know the Count, why did he go to her house?" "The question I am here to ask you, Lea." "To ask me—am I a sorceress, Henry?" "In so far as a sorceress is 'usually cleverer than her kind, yes." "Then you think—:oh, but it is -impossible, it is ridiculous."

She laughed aloud, forcing herself to the ilood as an instrument may be forced by cleverness to a note of discord foreign to it. I perceived now that slie had brought me to her house for curioeify's sake—not to tell me what she knew, but to ascertain the ex- tent of my own suspicions. The discovery maddened me. I could have caught her arms, and thrust her' down, and com- pelled her to confess. The torture she put upon me was as deliberate as the insult— and yet -I suffered both, for sake. "It is ridiculous," she repeated, "the same folly which sends a man to a woman when his trouble is a woman. I knew the Count; knew him as I have told you. Would he speak of every chit the atelier or the cabaret discovered for him? It is madness, Monsieur Henry. You know that I cannot help you" And yet you- invite me to your house?" "To offer you my sympathy, my friend- ship—to hear you tell me why you did this thing; you, who could have had a thousand friends among your own _people, to seek one out of the great caravanserai of irrespon- Bibles, to prate of her virtue, to fight for her, to marry her—is not a woman's curiosity justly provoked?" "And for curiosity's sake you sent for me to-day—pardon me, I shall answer that question for you. There was something be- yond curiosity, Madame d'Alen9on, there .was fear."

CHAPTER XXXIV.

[Henry Gastonard tells Paddy O'Connell of Wig visit to Madame Lea.] Hotel S. Paul, Paris,

October 24th, 19Cat Dear Paddy,—l had called upon Maclathe Lea before your letter reached me. This was done at Jules Farman's request, for, be sure, that which you were thinking was not forgotten by a man so able. I found Ma-clame alone in a nandsome apartment in OM Avenue Kleber. She is not changed a whit, is the same beautiful lan- guorous creature that we knew of old time. I told you in my last letter that there is Boise talk of a reconciliation between her and her husband. This I do hot believe, prefer- ring the view that the Captain's affections are temporarily engaged elsewhere. A man who is kind toward his own failings generally has some charity to spare for those of his wife. Possibly, the man is merely a philosopher—l do not make myself his judge; It is sufficient to say that Madame received me With great cordiality, impressed upon me the fact that we were alone and bade me open my heart to her. If I did not do this, be sure that my attitude Was by no means irrespon- sive. I had come to her house to learn if Madame Lea had a secret, and, learning that, to obtain it from her if that' were possible. A false word would have ruined all. I realised that Mimi's very life might depend upon suc- cess or failure; nor was I unaware that my own happiness might be won or lost in that very room. We. begin with a talk that was common- place enough—her health and mine, my de- parture from Paris, the absurdities of our last meeting—and so to Mimi and my marriage by a natural sequence which diplomacy de- manded. I found her eloquent immediately when Mimi's name was mentioned. A woman will discuss a man's love affair readily enough; there is no surer passport to his con- fidence, perhaps to his heart. And Lea d'Alencon, you will remember, speaks with little fluency upon any other subject. Imagine, Paddy, a considerable apartment furnished with all the precarious grace of the Louis XV. period but flauntingly modern and garish in its tone. Say that the walls are panelled in silk of a deep golden hue; put long mirrors wherever there are niches for them; place clocks of many kinds upon the tables and in the angles—Cupids marking the hour of the day; the Hesperides shouldering the golden apple; "Father Time treading Ones. beneath his giant feet " all the baubles which are sold in the Rue de la P_aix, and others which came God knows whence. The furniture itself was bought, I believe, at the last Exhibition. It is fine but new, oh, so new !—and Lea's gown of white and gold brocade is caught up by it as a flower of bi.-sarre magnificence suitable to ISO bright a bed. As for Madame, her eyes are as black as ever, her hair 84 aniendid—but think that

COTTON AND FREE TRADE. SUPREMACY OF THE BRITISH COTTON INDUSTRY.

The simplest evidence of the supremacy of the British cotton industry is found in com- paring the exports of cotton manufactures from this country with those of other countries.

Our competitors have open to them all the sources of raw material supply, and all the great markets for consumption of cotton manufactures, on the same terms as our- selves. There are no tariffs to exclude them from the United Kingdom, from India, or from our Crown Colonies. We have no com- petitive advantage, excepting, firstly, the old experience and skill of those engaged in the industry; and, secondly, our untaxed imports, which enable the trade to obtain every requisite material at the most _economical rates.

'A TARIFF-REFORM ANTICIPATION.

Tariff Reformers point out that in recent years the exports of cotton manufactures from foreign countries have increased more rapidly, in proportion, than our own. Thus, comparing the two years 1896 and 1906, the export values (in million pounds) were: 1896. 1900. Frcm Germany „ Holland 23 ... 4.3 „ Belgium „. France „ Switzerland „ Italy „ Spain 9.0 ... 11 „ Austria-Hungary OO ... 2.2 „ United States „ Japan

Total £26o ...165.8 From the United Kingdom At these rates of progress Tariff Reformers anticipate that in a very few years we shall have more than one equal rival in the cotton trade. But the percentage method of com- parison is apt to be misleading in cases like this. In those eleven years our competitors, taken all together, added £30,000,000 to their cotton exports. We added £33,000,000 to ours. So long as our rates of increase remain in that same proportion to theirs, they cannot, .even collectively, overtake us, much less can any one of them become an equal competitor. These foreign cotton industries are of com- paratively recent birth. They were stimu- lated at the outset by heavy protective tariffs and so grew with hothouse rapidity. It remains to be seen how they will fare through the intense competition of future years, when their workers press harder, as they are sure to do, for better conditions of life.

===== 0002601_19090515_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====
GROWTH OF THE COTTON TRADE.

The number of cotton spindles in the United Kingekm increased from 371 millions in 1874 to 501 millions in August, 1907. In the last-named year the total number of cotton- spinning spindles in the world was estimated at 114 millions; so that the United Kingdom had nearly one-half the total. The United States 'came next with 26 millions, and Ger- many third with 9 million's. In the same years there has been a great "speeding up" of the machinery. Some authorities put the increase of yarn output due to this cause alone during the last quarter of a century at 100 per cent. • a more moderate estimate,well on the safe side, is a 60 per cent. increase. Note that statistics of the weight of raw cotton consumed in the United Kingdom its compared with other countries are mislead- ing, because the yarn spun in this country is, on the average, much finer than in other countries.

The power looms in the British cotton trade increased ftom 463,000 in 1874 to 684,000 in 1903; or, roundly, 50 per cent. Since 1903 there has been a further increase, of which, however, precise figures are not available. The speed of looms, like that of spindles, has been continuously increased during many years, thus proportionately increasing the output.

===== 0002601_19090515_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
INCREASE OF EXPORTS.

The British exports of cotton piece goods increased continuously from an average of 3,647 million yards per annum in 1876-80 to 5,141 million yards per annum in 1896-1900. Thence there was a further increase to 6,298 million yards in 1907. In the depressed year 1908 the exports were still 5,532 million yards. Let us note, also, that the real pro- gress of the British cotton trade has been even larger than indicated by these figures ; because the quantities of the finer and more costly goods have increased more rapidly, whilst the quantities of the cheaper goods have diminished.

WHY FOREIGN COMPETITION FAILS. The most important cause of the failure of foreign competitors to meet us successfully is their higher working expenses, consequent on protective tariffs. In England, the building and equipment of a cotton-spinning factory cost 255. per spindle The cost of a factory .of the same size in Germany is 378. per spindle; in France 355. per spindle. In the United States the cost is 50s. per spindle. With standing capital expenses 50 to 100 per cent. higher than here, it is impossible for foreign manufacturers to export as cheaply as do we from this country. They must either charge higher prices or pay lower wages.

WHY P-ROTECTION WOULD HINDER OUR EXPORTS.

Careful estimates shew-that the 5 per cent. duties all round proposed by Tariff Reformers would increase by-L305 per annum the work- ing expenses of a cotton factory built at a cost of £27,600. Again, in a spinning mill of 80,000 spindles, costing £lOO,OOO, tho working expenses would be increalsed by £613 per annum. These sums may not seem large, but they are enough to mEi.ice the difference between profit and loss in the intense com- petition of the cotton export; trade. Only 20 per cent, of our cotton manufac- tures are consumed in tltis country. The other 80 per cent. are exported, and for the exports higher prices could not be obtained in face of foreign competition. LANCASI-lIRE'S OPINION.

Lancashire's hostile opinion on Tariff Reform Was unmistakably signified in the 1906 General Election. It is asserted that opinion in Lancashire has changed since then. Nevertheless, in February of this year (1909) a very fully attended meeting of the Manchester Chamber of Commerce rejected a proposition. favouring Tariff Reform, and passed by an overwhelming majority a resolution affirming that the maintenance of our pre sent Free Trade system is a vital necese;ity for the cotton industry.

===== 0002601_19090515_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
PIGS AT A PRIMUJI.

There is now something approaching a boon in pigs, writes "Old Breeder," in Farm and Home." Bacon is selling at a very fair price, more than 10s. per, score having been made lately of small "bacon pigs. Vet the trade forstores is slow, owing, it Is said, to the high price of feeding stuffs. The ney.- regulations of the Local Government Board have, from January Ist, stopped the importation of netrly three millions (sterling) worth of pork " trimmings," and there is room in the markets for nearly half a million more pigs. Accentuating this deficiency, the last returns show a falling off in the number of breeding sows of 29,525. The Examiner, Saturday; May 15, 1909.

The words "Behold I stand at the door and knock " reminded us of that great picture "The light of the world." The door of our hearts was barred. He asked admittance. He pleaded and asked that we would let him in. What did he demand ? He came in to be Lord of all that he may rule with an absolute monarchy, to control hll our th )ughts and actions. He came to claim as body, soul and spirit for His own. The Christianity we wanted to put before the children at the outset of life, was a life given up solely to the service of Christ, to put an end to sin, sorrow, and disappointment. To those in later life, if we had followed Jesus as we ought let us pray that He with his loving kindness would come in and sup with us that the peace which cometh may be everlesting life. A duet entitled "Who is the Stranger" was sung ith much feeling by Misses B. Dutton and Olive Gladding. 'The collactions amounted to £l9 ss. Oct., in addition to which was an amount of El 4s. 03. collected by the scholars in penny subscriptions making a gross total of ,E 23 9s. Od. Any surplus left over after all expenses have been paid will go towards• the build- ing fund of the new Sundty School.
