Be trusted that the cluhmight be of the greatest value to those living in the district, and that the discipline and the training would have a beneficial, effect upon all concerned. (Applause). Sir Gilbert Greenall fired the first shot, Lady Greenall the second shot and Mrs. Shepherd-Cross the third shot. A hearty vote of thanks was accorded to Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall and Mr. Shepherd Cross, on the proposition of the Rev. T. T. Lee,-Jones, who said that although he was a parson and supposed to be a man of peace and to preach peace throughout all his sermons, he should like to say that he cordially agreed with what Sir Gilbert Greeuall had said, and that was that he believed the way to have peace was to be prepared for war. (Applause). In the course of a few years Englishmen might be so harassed by their neighbours across the water that it would be absolutely necessary for every man, woman and child to handle a gun to keep their homes from invasion.

Lymm. DEATH OF MR. JAMES GREGSON.

The death took place on Monday last of Mr. James Gregson, at his residence, " Loconby," Lymm. Mr. Gregson was a gentleman who in his day bad taken his full share of the public work of the district. He had been an active member of tho old Local Board and was the first Chairman of the Urn District Council when the old Order gave way to the new. He wai a governor of Lymm Grammar School, a director of the Conservative Club, and a prominent Freemason. Originally a school- master, he was educated at Chester Training College, from which profession he retired thirty years ago. He came from Denton to Lymm as head of Lymm school. He successfully engaged later in business, being con- nected with Lymm Mill and other undertakings. The deceased gentleman was 77 sears of age.

Culcheth.

SALE OF VALUABLE PROPERTY AT CULCII ET!' AND Lowros.—Messrs. R. -Greenohgh and S .ns were instructed by th executors of the late Mr. J. Rutter, of Culcheth, to sell by public auction at the White Horse Hotel, Railway-roa,l, Leigh, on Wednesday last, the freehold dwelling-house Lnown as Manor House, front- ing Twiss Green-lane, Culcheth, and nine freehold cottages, six situated in Twies-green-lane, and three off Twiss Green-lane. The lot realized £1,300. in addition to the above a freehold-farm at Lowton known as Bamber Lodge Farm, was sold and realized £2,250, and another farm situated on Landside, Leigb, known as Closes Farm, also realized £1,400. PIP RROT- TROUPE CONCERT.—A grand concert was given in the Newchureh schools on Saturday last by the Twiss Green pierriot Troupe. The troupe proved. a huge success at a bazaar recently held in the village, and the success was well maintained on Saturday. Bearing in mind the strictly amateurconstitntion of the .troupe and the restricted area from which it draws its talent (most of the members being connected with the Methodist Chapel) the performance could not be regarded as other than praiseworthy. :Ile jokes were mostly original and witty 2nd the songs were of a character whieh would suit any temperament. There was a large end appreciative audience which spoke well of the popularity of the troupe. Songs were sung by Miss Kenyon, who very tastefully rendered "Mabel and Joe," and Miss Barton was equally successful in "By the old Sundial," for which she was encored. Miss Howard who possesses a sweet voice also sang with much feeling "Pal of Mine" and Miss Elsie Willcocks was well rewarded with an encore for bar excellent singing of "My Pietrot " Miss E. Runcorn also achieved a considerable amount of snocess for the singing of" Strike up the band " and "Hello who's there " receiving a well deserved encore for both items. The songs ri Razors in the air" by Mr E. Barton, and" Orange Blossom " and "Hazel Dell" by Mr. H. Overton were also sung very expressively and an equal amount of praise is due to Mr. W. J. Bate for his singing of "Marching threugh Georgia" "Shine, shine Moon " " Three for Jack." Mr. J. R. Fletcher sang in a pleasing and artistic manner "When .the guards go by " and " Dinah said Yah "and for the latter item received an encore. The applause which greeted Mr. E. Overton's rendering of " Let me sing," and" Fiddle-de-om-pom " was well deserved and the originality of the latter provided much amusement. Equally amusing were the renderings of the " A.B.C. duet" by Messrs. Fletcher and E. Overton and "My Motor Car "by Messrs; E. and H.Overton. A humorous trio entitiscl " I would, I would " was very ably ren- dered by Messrs. Kenyon, Fletcher, and E. Overton and Mr. J. Sutton sang "Things that a man can't do" and "Can't think of everything" with befitting guests." Mr. W. Kenyon infused a great deal of life • into "De lecture " " Can't you take my word" and "Pierrot Band " his ludicrous mannerisms producing roars of laughter. A word of praise is due to Miss L. Ward for the excellent manner in which she accompanied the soloists during the evening. The proceeds of the concert which were to defray expenses ceused by the formation of the troupe realized about XL

Stockton Heath. WESLEY AN SUNDAY SCHOOL ANNIVERSARY.

The Sunday schoolsanniversary in connection with the above place of wofthip took place elast week end, when a visit was paid by the Rev. G. tr. Carnsoo, of Preston, and formerly of this circuit. The singing of the children and choir throughout all the services evidenced the excellent training they had received at the hands of Mr. F. E. Penketh. In the morning special hymns were sung by the children, and the preacher gave an eloquent and ingfructive sermon. A large congregation assembled in the afternoon to hear a service of song entitled" A Watered Garden." Miss Prince, an accomplished elocutionist was the reaaler,and the soloists were the Misses May Isherwood, Edith Howes and May Miller. The Mayor who presided said that it was the first time he had presided over a service in that Church, and he was very pleased indeed to see it filled. He must, first of all, congratulate them upon the excellent choir and upon the beautiful singing of the little children. That was a children's service, and he was always pleased to associate himself with anything that tended to the up-bringing of the young. It teemed to him that the fact of having those children's services carried out the scriptural injunction "Train up a child in the way it should go, and when he is old he will not depart from it." Those words he thought ought to be impressed upon the minds of every parent. During the evening service the choir rendered the anthem, "Behold God is my. salvation," whilst Mr. G. Radcliffe took the solo part. Miss Bancroft sang very sweetly "My heart ever faithful." The -Rev. G. Carnson look es his text "$0 mightilyarew the word of God and prevailed." (Acts xix. 20-.) In the course of his sermon he said the chief blessing that the Church and the people needed was the gift of the Holy Spirit. When Paul came to the 'Jews and the Greeks and asked about the Holy Spirit, they answered that they had never heard of it. The preacher was afraid a good many English people would have to say the same thing, if they were asked the question. Some would have to say: "Yes, we board of it, but we grieved it, and it has gone." Somesaid they bad never been touched in their hearts by that gift of the Holy Spirit. They should have done with that kind of thing and go into the work of God. There was not a Church in the Kingdom that did not need some help. They should not spend their money on foolish things; they sliould spend it on God's wot k. Referring to the history of the men who were converted through the influence of Paul he said that the men had burned books to the value of £2,000. When men were converted they gave up many things which they conidered sin. Some had j burned up footballs, violins and cards too. They ' thought, it s'.n t o,lteep them and they had been all the better for it. If they even found their pipes to be s'n they should burn them. If some of them were to be converted they would have to put tile7r roller skates in , the fire. The girls of Preston were beginning to take their trinkets and their jewels to the pawnbroker s and selling them for any price so that they might go to the rink. It was already beginning to be a curse, and he prayed God it Would stop. Concluding, he said that as soon as .the Christian Church had the gift of the spirit of God she would prosper. The collections for the day amounted to .21.4 4s. 4d. On Monday evening the Rev. E. B. Keeling gave a lecture on " Words about words," Mr. IL H. Meldrum presided and in a few remarks complimented the children upon their singing and the conductor (Mr. F. E. Penketh) on the admirable way in which they had been taught. The work that they at Stockton Heath were doing , was very good and needed to be encouraged. The/Rev. E. B. Keeling in the course of Ns lecture which was decidedly humorous and instructive said that many wr rds had changed their meaning very contider- ably. He gave an account of some of the words used in years gone by, and instanced some of the words that had altered in their meaning. Mr. Deakin in moving a vote of thanks to the lecturer and the chairman, said that Mr. Meldrum ws always willing to help them at fitockton Heath, when- ever help was Wanted. He- had greatly enjoyed Mr. Keeling's lectura and he hoped to hear him on some future occasion. The speaker coupled with the resoluti(aa thechoir, and said they were always willing to help them, and for that they owed them a debt of grit itude. The motion was carried with applause.

Daresbury. OPENING OF THE RIFLE RANGE.

The range of the newly-formed Daresbury Rifle Club, was formally opened on Saturday afternoon in charming weather by Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart. Ideally situated in the stone delph, the range is open to all residents in the townships of Daresbury, Preston Brook, Hatton, Higher Walton, Acton Grange, Moore, Keel:wick, i Norton and Newtoh-by-Daresbury, but it s stipulated that the members' must be over 14 years of age. The Executive Committee have the en ire charge of the range during practice, and no firing is to allowedunless one of the members is present. The range is to be open until September 30th, on Tuee.zday evenings, from six to 8.30, and on Saturdays from three to 8.30, and at any other time by arrangement with a member of the committee. The distances for shootinrare 25 yards, 50 yards, and 100 yards. Amongst those present at the opening ceremony were :—Lady Greens'', the Rev. T. T. Lee-Jones and Mrs. Jones, Mr. and Mrs. C. Shepherd-Cross, Mr. and Mrs. J. Whitley, Mr. John White, Mr. and Mrs. R. I. Wynne Jones, Mr. J. E. Becket, Mr. aid Mrs. W. Bleckly, Mrs. Kirkconnel, Mr. and Mrs. John Whitlow, Mr. A. Broome, Mr. and Mrs. John Wright, and Mr. H. F. Woods. Mr. Shepherd-Cross said the number of members at present who had paid their subscriptions as 122-98 men and 24 ladies, and they bad received in donations towards formation expenses £27 Sr. and a grant from the Astor fund of rifles a nounting to £5 Os., making £32 145., which, he thought, was a very satisfactory. , kite. In addition to that they 'had the annual sub- scriptions the ordinary member' subscriptions and the promise of the same subscriptions for years to come of £l4 75., so that they were starting as well as any club could hope to expect. (Applause.) i Sir Gilbert Greenall, n opening the range, said the rifle movement had his most cordial support, not only because it was is means of establishing bodies of men

THE FUNERAL.

The funeral of the deceased .sentleman took place yesterday at the Lymm Church, when a number of people assembled to show their respect for their late townsman. From the house to the church a procession of the prominent men of the town marched in front of the cortege. Arrived at the church, the Revs. G. B. Thurston and. Edwardes Evans conducted the service. As the procession slowly moved down the aisle Mr. Cooke played " 0 rest in the Lord," and at the conclusion of the service he rendered the Dead March in Saul." The choir boys were in attendance and the hymn" 0 God our help in ages past," as well as two others were very effectively sung. The chief mourners were as follows: Ist coach, Messrs. T. T. THE MAY QUEEN FESTIVAL.—At the meeting of the Committee on Tuesday night, reference was made to the loss sustained by the death -of Mr. James Gregson, and a sympathetic vote of condolence with the family of the deceased gentleman was passed, on the proposition of Mr. J. D. B. Smith (chairman.)-;--Reports from the various sub-committees were submitted, and disclosed a satisfactory progress of arrangements. With regard to the tradesmen's turnouts—for which class Mr. G. H. Wood, of Altrincham, has consented to act as judge— it was decided to proceed with this part of the proces- sion half an hour earlier than the main body, in order to obviate the possibility of any accident occuring during the judging and subsequent display of the turn- outs round the field.

LYMM PRIMITIVE METHODIST ANNIVERSARY.—The Sunday school anniversary in connection with the above place of worship was held last weekend. ' Mr. Woodall, a late pastor, was the preacher. Special hymns were sung by the children, who showed the Skilful training they had received from Mr. T. M. Easton." Miss F. E. Shore was the organist. In the morning prior to the sermon, Mr. Woodall gave an interesting address to the children, and in his sermon he said that the influence that the parents tore over their children was far greater than the influence of the schools, or of their everyday association. It behoved the parents to see that fheir influence- was worthy of imitation. The anthem" Come unto Me "was sung,the leading part being taken by Miss Appletcn.—The after- noon serv:cs was a " Red letter" one for the children, and thsy sung for ell they were worth. Recitations were given by the Misses E. Atherton, It Wibberley, A. ROvvies, B.Cross, J.Thornicroft and Meter A.Thiestmer. Mr. Welford presided and Mr. Woodall gave a short address.—At the evening service Mr. Woodall spoke on work in the service of Christ. CITIZENS' SUNDAY. TR XDESNIEN'S NC N IC.

The Lymm Tradesmen's Association met on Monday -night last, Mr. P. E. Evans presiding. It was decided to ask the Rector of Lyrists] to preach the sermon on Citizens' Sunday,the date to be in October. The Rev.E.P., Wethered, Rector of Oughtrington,had written suggest- ing the holding of the festival this yeas/ in his Church, but the meeting considered it would be more convenient to hold it in Lymm Church. The meeting considered the cultization . of the social side of the work of the Association, and it was -decided to holdla pie-lie to Rhyl some time in July.—The President communicated- the result of his correspondence with the Postal Authorities regarding the unfortu- nate placing, of the poles carrying the wires ever the village cross from which it appears that they disclaimed responsibility in the matter as the Post Office Engineer did not fix upon the positions without first consulting the members of the District Council. If satisfactory arrangements could be made one pole would be moved to a position which would have the effect of taking the wires from over the top of the cross. This was considered by the members to be only a partial settlement and further action will be taken to have the whole removed to some less noticeable route. The thanks of the members were extended to Mr. Evans for the trouble be had taken in the Matter. '

Grappenhall.

SALE OF WORK.

We reported in our coltimns last week the particulars connected with the opening proceedings of the sale of work held last Friday. The effort was continued on Saturday and opaned in the presence of a very con- siderable number of members and friends.

After singing and prayer, Mr. James Kenworthy expressed his great pleasure in being present and desired to say a word or two by way of encouragement to the members and workers of the church. He gave a most interesting survey of various kinds of work and particularly emphasized the fact that the church in whose aid they were met formed a part of that Methodist body which had been staunch temper- ance workers ever since its inception and claimed to be the pioneers of the Total Abstinence Movement. He traced the growth of the present church from an upper room in the village and trusted that the same spirit and success which attended the labours of those who formed the early church in the upper room at Jerusalem might come upon their church. He understood the present building was inadequate for the growing needs of the neigbourhood and that within the next few years they were hoping to obtain the means to erect a more suit- able church and be'''expressed his readiness to help them in any possible way to promote the welfare of God's cause in the village. He then called upon Mrs. F. R. Roberts to open the sale of work. Mrs. Roberts said that she also was pleased to be with them that afternoon, and to do what little she was able to encourage and help them in their effort. She said she had teen to many sales of work and bazaars and invariably came away feeling that the speeches had been too long, and determined that, i f ever she was privileged to open one she would try to avoid making a long speech. She wished to say that they had her hearty sympathy, and hoped they would be'even more successful than on the previous day. After a vote of thanks to Mrs. Roberts and the aairman bad bcen proposed and heartily received,Miss A. Leah gave a pianoforte solo. The sale was then proceeded with and we are pleased to report that the results exceeded the anticipation of the workers who thankfully acknowledge the help they have received in many directions and also wish to express their thanks to the ladies of the village and neighbourhood for their generous support. The net preceeds realized et SG.

Warrington Children's Summer Camp. Sir,—.on Saturday last a number of poor men and boys associated with the Slum 11Ii=sion at Notting Dale, London, were entertained by the boys at Rugby School. Commenting on the event the Bishop of London, who was present, laid great stress on the Al stlikim comparison between the physical condition and tibvelorment of the Rugby boys and the poor boys who came from the slums. He remarked that it ' made him ,feel other than particularly happy about the state of the nation. It was really touching to see the dwarfed, underfed, undersized, boys and men who came from the slums, and was it to be wondered at that such b.cgs grew up utterly unfitted to take their proper place in the army of workets ? It is this physical poiLt of view that we would impress on your reade)s' minds. To counteract the influences which poor children who are compelled to live in unwhole- some and insanitary homes labour under we appeal to

your readers. The Committee of the Children's Camp are exceed- ingly anxicus this year to send a larger number of poor gi,ls and boys to Prestatyn, as the Canal.; is in splendid working order. The money ,is coming in somewhat slowly ! May we urge upon them the con- sideration of the sate in which the poor children are placed ? Helpless to help themselves, surrounded as :a rule by those who from varying circumstances are unable to help them. •It is very desirable to put up Caretakers' Quarters this season. Will they help to do this? Every shilling is of importance! We would ask all thcse who have the welfare of their country at heart to lend a hand in the work of recruiting and building up the physical condition of thcse who are unable to help themselves.— Yours &c., J. A Tariff Reform and Leather. Sir,-The report on the fii -t of the Warrington open-air Tariff Reform meetings has been sent to me by one of my Warrington tanner friends who asks me -what I think of Mr. Tom Smith's reference to the leather and bide trade. If Mr. Tom Smith is correctly reported I should say he is absolutely ignorant, as the quantity, of Birkishead hides which have been re-exported to the United States is exceedingly srll, o also is the quantity of leather (made from that class of hides) sent here by the United States, and as to Mr. Smith's statement that the "Americans make John Bull pay through the ncse for the finished leather" this is utter, rubbish. I think this is the first time I have beard a Tariff Reformer complain of us having to "pay through the nose" for any manafactured article; the cry is that these manufactured articles are dumped down here at less than cost price. In past years a great deal of AmeriCan leather was dumped down here, and possibly Mr. Tom Smith bought it. then in preference to the home made pro- duct, and his reference to paying "through the nose" for .American leather may refer to the fact that American tanners can no longer dump cheap leather here as they used to do, and Mr. Smith resents paying full prices for these finished products. Mr. Smith may put cheap American leather out of his mind for the present and like a good loyal subject confine him- self to home made goods. The ccmplexity and the vastness of the interests involved in the Voids Exports, Imports, Taxation, Duties, Protection, renders it particularly necessary that these who stand up to instruct others should be conversant with all their facts. If we are to have Tariff Reform by all' means let us understand what we are about. Dees Mr. Smith want a duty placed on imported leather, or a duty placed on exported hides, or does he want the United States to send us their cattle without hides ?-I(curs, EDWARD PIM.

14, Dale-street, Diverpool.

Tom Smith, of Burnley, stated at the Tariff Reform meeting. last week that "In 1005-6 the bide of every Least that came to Birkenhead lairage to be Elaughtei et] for meat was stripped (.ff and sent back to America to be tanned, dressed, and finished there, and then they made us pay through the nose fcr the -finish«l lather." Mr. Smith's statement is entirely false. During the two years -mentioned I tanned over 30,000 of these Anglo-American hides and other tanners tanned similar quantities. Surely one of the first requisitiea of a public speaker is that he should speak the truth. Mr. Smith has apparently thought that with an audience not likely to be conversant with the real facts he could foist any untruth upon them with- out being brought to . book. It is difficult to know what the man is driving at. It is however scarcely worth while answering the muddled argument rh. n the facts upon which it is based are false. CHARLES E. PARKER. Penketh Tannery, May 13th, 1909. • Mr. Parker's Reply to Critics. . Sir,—Your correspondents Avlio have kindly criticized my paper make reference to the statement of Chiozza Money in which he ,says that the monopoly of caPital is a far more reaching thing than the monopoly of land, and that British land rents are in comparison insignifi- cant. I entirely demur to Mr. Money's conclusions. Be bases his opinions upon the figures of Schedules A and D in the Income Tax Returns, subject to certain corrections. I contend this basis is altogether mis- leading and perfectly uSeless in considering the economic effect of land monopoly. The effect of the land monopoly is not made manifest to any appreci- able extent in the divisions of ,the income tax returns. It is in the main buried in Schedule D. Mr. Money's guess about the apparent land rent of the United Kingdom, £100,000,000, may be as near the 'truth as any such guesses usually aredikut he aktogether ignores the far more important fa of the advantage which the possessor of land acquires as a capitalist through the possession of monopoly. The income tax in Schedule D which Mr. Money would attribute to the earnings of capital is to a great extent the earnings— if they could be called earnings—of land and ;other monopolies. Probably there is no industry which in the past twenty years has yielded a higher return than the brewing industry. This has arisen not through the work of capital, but is due to the acquisitionOf a monopoly in sale created by the State, for what is deemed to be for the social benefit of the people. This illustrates the point in my paper that when capital can exchange its rights for the monopoly it is a danger. It confirms what I " tried to emphasize about capital entrenching itself behind monopolies. In the same way Schedule D which Mr. Money looks upon as the earnings of capital, contains the advantages which arise from proprietory monopoly rights of patents ; these • Koprietory rights of inventionthave no doubt some justification, but they are no capital, and the profits arising from them should not be classed as capital earnings. Further in Schedule D there i&-no deduction made for lost capital. Mr. Money in another part of his book speaks of the loss of hundreds of millions of capital during the last twenty years (page 152) but he refers specially to the formation of bogus companies. The loss of capital in these may be as great as Mr. Money vaguely states although I .doubt it, still he makes no deduction for it, but the more important item which Mr. Money ignores is the loss of capital in legitimate trading, and Mr. Money's estimate of the earnings of capital only recognizes the profits and ignores the losses. His process is something like that of a trader who if he made £2,000 gross and £l,OOO bad debts still represented his profits as £2,000. Schedule D entirely omits the deduction which ought to be made for the capital lost in undertakings by Companies and individuals which have been obliged to *rite .down their capital, to wind up; or suspend payment. But the most important point which Mr. Money over looks in estimating capital from an economic standpoint, is the advantage which capital can gain by monopolizing some natural product, force, or position, and this is the point which appears to me of the greatest importance in the question I have raised of the nation exercising control over natural things.

NOTICE TO CORIIESPONDENTS.! 04, J.C.W.—We regret that we are unable to find room for S-our letter this week.—En. "Ex."

===== 0002601_19090515_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
Mr. Ramsay Macdonald, speaking at Bristol the other day upon the Budget, said he wanted the money to be raised in such a way that the burden upon land, uneatned increment, upon monopoly profits would become heavier, at the same time that the burden upon earned incomes, upon industrial capital, upon industrial charges of all kinds would become less. That was the problem before them, and it would be seen from that why they were Free. Traders. Free Trade was a. good circumstance for evolution towards Socialism. tinder Tariff Reform and Pro- tection all industrial sections of the community would bear, a heavy weight." The italics 4„re mine, I emphit i size the words used to show that n this matter at least Mr. Macdonald and Myself are entirely at one. I want the 'profits arising from monopoly' to .be claimed by those who create the value, and the burdens on real capital to be lessened. These monopoly profits are returned under Schedule D and Mr. Money entirely ignores them. It is true he makes an attempt to estimate some of them on the basis of assessment', but surely he must know that in the assessment it is quite impossible to calculate them. These monopoly earn- ings all go to the credit of capital, whereas in the., true economic sense they are earned by the possession of the land, or other kinds of monopoly. This brings me in accord with the statement in " Democrat's " letter (although I demur to the loose way in which the word capital is used) "as long as there is a practical monopoly of material things it inevitably follows that there must be a practical monopoly of the -capital." • I 'therefore contend that Mr. Money's conclusion is invalidated when we closely examine it. it will be seen by any who wink carefully think the question out that the rise in building land in urban districts to which special attention is now directed, while an important factor in this question, is after all only a minor one, and a ,much more important aspect is revealed when we consider the advantages which, capital obtains when tinder our present system it is able to secure the benefit of monopoly. Glasgow Corporation had to pay £175,000 for the site of a municipal building which a hundred years ago had been sold for £BOO. For 431 acres of swamp land they had to , pay £l,OOO per acre, which land had probably cost the original owner. say £lO an acre. Now the owner to which this unearned increment had gone would not pay anything in income tax because it would be con- sidered as an aPprecitition'of capital and therefore not taxable, and incidentally this shows the justification of some of the 'features of the present Budget. But supposing some trading concern had bought this land and used it for manufacturing purposes the earnings of this capital would be included for purpose of income tax in class D. but the part which was due to the advantage of position 'ought by rights to have been included in land earnings, and these earnings are by no means represented by the amount paid for the land. There are a number of Other interesting points raised in your correspondent's letters but I have no wish to run into three columns. In the above .1 have dealt with -the main issue and the most important from my point of view as expressed in the paper under criticism. _

- • CHARLES E. PARKER, Penketh. May 10th, 1609.

THE FOOTBALL CLUB.

BUSINESS FOR THE ANNUAL

MEETING.

A Sound Financial Position.

The annual meeting of the Rugby Football Club will be held on Thursday evening next in the Bold-street School. The reports and statement of accounts which have been circulated among the 1083 members are full oi interesting information. Mr. H. Ashton, the hon. secretary, reviews the season's play at considerable length, and touches upon the difficulties which beset the Club at the opening of he season. These sere chiefly brought about by the unwillingness of some of the Welsh players to turn up in time for either preparation or the earlier matches. Mr. Ashton adds: The standard of play has been of a very high order throughout the season, and if not of a consistent character has clearlY indicated our claims to still being acknowledged as an organization of the first order in the Rugby world. . . . . Our attack requites more sting to be effective, and with the apparent weakening in that respect by several of our playing strength, such essential requirements rcust be faced in no half-hearted manner—at any sacrifice—by the executive appointed for the next term. To get class players is a task now becoming of greater magni- tude than ever; so many clubs are on the look out, creating more competition, hence the supply is becom- ing smaller. However, the ensuing playing period demands a more aggressivec policy if success is to be achieved, or even if our position is to be maintained." References to the promising outlook of junior football in the Works, and Schoolboy football conclude an interesting report. Mr. Pemberton, the hon. treasurer, reports that "The membership shews a slight increase over past years, viz., 1.083. Negotiations for a renewal of the lease for ten sears from the Ist October, 1208, have been satisfactorily carried out, the lease being signed on behalf of the members by Messrs. John Evans, T. Locker, H. Ashton and myself. The Directors of Messrs. Greenall, Whitley end Co. Ltd. kindly promised to give an annual donation of £lO. The net gate for 1908-9 is £2,008 13s. Bd., against £1,840 10s. 10d. Considerable sums of money have been expended in transfer fees and the signing on of new players. Refreshments have increased owing to the tour in South Wales. The sports and the bar have produced larger profits than last season. Arrangements were made to insure the players against accidents with an Insurance Company, I?ut were after- wards cancelled. This has since proved a good gain to the Club, owing to fewer claims having been made by players. The terms under which the Warrington Bill- posting Company erected the fence at the railway end expired last year, and your Committee purchased the same for £4O. The sum of £B6 Bs. was raised from the Works Competition, and grants to the Infirmary 30 guineas, Nurses' Association 20 guineas, St. John Ambulance Society 7 guineas, have beet' made; and' recompense has been made to injured players. Watches and medals have been provided for the Schools Com- petition. e Among the items detailed are : remuneration of the secretary, treasurer, and auditors, with clerical assist- ance, £173 4s. ; gates shared with other teams £360 12s. 5d.; rents, rates, taxes etc., £145 125.; wages of players, bonuses and transfers, £1,381 10s. ; refreshments and travelling expenses, £159 is. 10d. ; Doctors' fees, etc., £59 Gs. 11d. ; payments to injured players, £llB us. ; bar payments £lB7 6s. 10d. On the credit side of the ledger are : Cash in bank and hand, £613 2s. 7d.; bar stock and debtors, £5O 16s. 4d.; entrance fees and subscriptions, £391 10s. ; sports profit, £43 us. ; gate money, Ist team, £1,795 4s. ; A team, £322 13s. 6d. ; Works Competition. £B6 Bs. ; insurance claims, £6B ss. The abstract of accounts shows the assets of the Club, after ample depreciation, to be £1,615 Bs. 4d. ; and the liabilitiew£lo I." 55., leaving a balance of £1,514 Sc. 4d.

MARRIED WOMEN TEACHERS.

Notices Withdrawn in Three Cases.

The School Management Committee of the Warring- ton Education Committee has considered communica- tions received. in respect of certain married women teachers affected by the Committee's regulations dealing with such teachers, as follows, viz :—From Mrs. Mary Dooley, sewing mistress in the mixed department of the Latcbford St. James' C.E. school; from the managers of the S. Alban's R.C. school, forwarding particulars supplied by Mrs. Mary Gittoes, certified assistant mistress in the mixed department of that school,•? from Nrs. Emily Constance Scarisbrick, certi- licatedassistant mistress in the infants' department of Wycliffe school; and from Mrs. Langdon Isabel Cann, superintendent of cookery instruction. After careful consideration of these communications it was resolved that the reasons submitted by or on behalf of Mrs. Mary Dooley, Mrs. Mary Gittoes,' and Mrs. Emily Constance Scartsbrick be accepted as special reasons within the meaning of regulation 3of ' the regulations in question and that their services be continued. It was resolved that three months notice from the 31st day of May, 1909, be given to Mrs. Langdon Isabel Cann to terminate her engagement as Superintendent of Cookery Instruction on the 31st August, 1909. Ihe committee accepted Mrs. Elizabeth Hankinson's resignation of her position as a certificsted assistant mistress in the senior mixed department of the St. Ann's school, and it was resolved that resolution No. C 2471 be rescinded; and that the notice Riven therein to Mrs. Elizabeth Hankinson to terminate her engage- ment as a certificated assistant •mistress in the senior mixed department of the St. Ann's school be withdrawn.

The Examiner, Saturday May 15, 1909.

DEATH OF ALDERMAN ELLISON, J.P.

Warrington has lost another of its most respected public Men by the. death of Alderman Luke. Ellison, J.P., who passed away at his residence, 681, linuts- ford-road, on Monday afternoon at 3.45. To most of the townspeople the news of his death came as a painful shock, for few were even aware that he was unwell. So recently as Tuesday evening of last week Alderman Ellison attended a meeting of the Town Council and on arriving home about ten o'clock he complained not only of the exceptionally long sitting —over three.kours—but of the heated debates, which had somewhat: upset him, and he retired to rest not feeling very well. The excitement appears to have brought on an attack of his old complaint, heart trouble, but no danger was anticipated until Saturday evening when he became worse. Not until .Sunday afternoon, however, was it deemed necessary to procure medical aid, and Dr. Robinson was called in and attended him up to the time of his death. A few Minutes before he died he asked someone in his employ if Ile Was ready to go home after his day's work and the _reply was., "Yes." To this the Alderman replied "Soam I. lam going home." These were his last words. The deceased leaves a widow and two sons and two daughters. The news of his death created widespread regret, for his kindly, sympathetic, unostentatious manner, as well as the excellent religious and social work which he did, had endeared him to all classes of the community.

It is a pathetic circumstance that only a few weeks ago, Alderman Ellison contributed the. story of his own life to the "Examiner." He had been associated with Warrington. -and the immediate district from childhood. He was born at the village of Maitinscroft on March 18th, 1836, and was bought up in very humble circumstances. .His father, Thomas Ellison, was a hand-loom weaver up to his twenty-fifth year, but the advent of machinery caused .him to turn his

===== 0002601_19090515_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LATE ALDERMA Y ELLISON,

attention to labouring. He removed to Latchford two years after his son Luke was born and was employed on the Old Warps fa rm,,now Victoria Park, for over twenty years. Alderman Ellison had few educational advantages, his instruction being received first at an old dame school and afterwards at the Primitive Methodist Sunday School in Old Road. At the age of ten he went to work in the fields along with his father, but a year later he was apprenticed to the boot trade with a Mr. Bostock, of Bridge-street. After serving there for two :-ears he was transferred to Mr. Newall's, the Ackers,. Latchford, where he completed his apprenticeship. By the time he had 'attained his majority he had commenced business on his own accotmt in Knutsford-road, and continued with gratify- ing success up to the time of his death. Alderman Ellison formed a link with the early . days when Latchford was an old-fashioned country village and nothing delighted him more than to talk of the great changes which had occurred during his life-time both in Latchford and in Warrington. One of the greatest delights of Alderman Ellison!s life was Sunday school work with which he had been actively associated from an 'early age. First he was identified with the Primitive Methodist denomination but when the Wesleyans formed a Society in Latch- ford he threw in his lot with them. In the meantime he had some share in the building of Christ Church, Latchford, and he had the pleasure of helping the late Rev. Richard Greenall there for a short time. He was twenty-six years of age when he joined the Wesleyan Methodists and he proved himself such a conscientious and vigilant worker that he was honoured by election to all the lay offices, including those of Sunday school superintendent, .Chapel steward, treasurer and trustee. Two years ago he relinquished Sunday school work, feeling that advancing rears and long service demanded that he should, take a rest. To use his own words :—" Nothing has afforded me greater satisfaction than.to see the progress of the Wesleyan Church in this neighbourhood. We have,now.a large and well appointed Sunday school and the opening of the new organ in the church was perhaps the crown- ing achievement," - -

Alderman Ellison was warmly attached to the temperance movement and took a special interest in the Independent Order of Good Templars. In 1873 Ile formed the •' Bonehill " branch lodge at Latch- ford, the meetiggs being held in an old barn on the site of the present Wesleyan -Church. A juvenile lodge was also started, and he had been the superin- tendent of this from the commencement, at the same time holding various other offices connected with the society. In the erection of the Temperance Hall and of the Wesleyan Church Mr. Ellison took an active part. In 1860 he joined the " Mount Hermon" Lodge of the Ancient Order, of Foresters.

Mtisic was one of Alderman Ellison's chief hobbies, and he took part in the formation of the Lrtchford Brass Band. He served as band sergeant for six years in the first company of local Volunteers under Captain Greenall, and he also was five years in the 4th Lancashire Militia Band, under Colonel Black- burne, the headquarters of which were at the old barracks in Orford-street.

On leaving the band Alderman Ellison was appointed the first sub-postmaster of Latchford, and held the position for twentyseven years. From his youth he had taken an interest in municpal affairs. He could remember walking the borough boundary with the first Mayor (Mr. William Beamont) in 1847 and he had personally known every Mayor that the .borough has had. It was not until 1895, however, that he sought municipal honours and then at the request of many of the inhabitants he agreed to contest Latchford Ward in the Liberal interest. Latchford was then a Conservative stronghold, but such was Alderman Ellison's personal popularity that he defeated Mr. Linnaeus Greening by sixty-two votv —much to the surprise of the , Conservatives who had held the seat for 27 years. Alderman Ellison never lost the confidence of his constituents, and although he had to fight for his seat at three succeeding intervals of three years, he was triumphant on each occasion. No-one derived more satisfaction from Council work, into which he threw himself whole- heartedly, but probably the department in which he took most interest was the School Attendance Com- mittee, over Which he presided with every considera- tion for the poor people who often had toappear before him. A little over twelve months ago the Council recognized his faithful services by electing him to an aldermanic seat. Alderman Ellison was a staunch Liberal even in the days when to be so required no little courage, and he took a pleasure in recalling many of the great political fights of bye-gone days. He was, however, esteemed by men of all parties for his many sterling qualities and his death creates a gap which it will indeed be difficult to fill. Mr. and Mrs. Ellison celebrated their golden wedding on Sunday, February 7th, and were the recipients not only of a large number of presents, but of nnmerous congratu- latory letters and telegrams from all parts of the country. -

Iq.GISTERIAL REFERENCES. , Before the commencement of the , business at the Borough Police Court, on Tuesday, Alderman Bolton (who presided), said he had a very painful duty to per- form. He understood that one of their best magis- trates, and one of his colleagues on the Town Hall, Parks and Baths Committee, had passed away. He had been most useful as an alderman and magistrate, and his death would be a great loss to Warrington, and the magistrates in general. Alderman Ellison had done his duty in that Court and as far as he could to theinhabi- tants of Warrington. ft bad been his duty to look after the parks and part of Victoria Park, and he (Alderman Bolton) could certainly say that they could look -back' with satisfaction upon what Alderman Ellison had done. It had been a shock to him that he had passed away so quickly. He would like to send a message of condolence to the family.

Mr.X. Jackson Holmes sail he could assure the Court that no one was held in greater respect than the late Aliermin Ellison.

Mr. Browne, on behalf of the solicitors practising in the Court, said he concurred with all that had been said. Alderman Ellism hal lived in Latchford all his life, and he was 'respected by everyone. He had been a most painstaking magistrat3.

THE FUNERAL.

Striking testimony te the respect in which the deceased gentleman was held was afforded by the large attendance, representative of all classes, at the funeral which •to ek place in the peaceful churchyard at Grappenhall, on Thursday afterno en. The Mayor, members of the Town Council and Educatioa Committee and Borough magis- trates assembled at the Town "Thal and proceeded in special tram cars b Latchford Weslayaa church where a service was held. The cars, which were draped in black, were in charge of Cttief Inspector Quinn. The church WAS filled to overfloing and the service; which was conducted by the Rev. John G innell, was deeply impressive. lhe hymns "0 Gad our help in ages past' and Give me the wings of faith to rise" were sung, Mr. Frank Hickman presiding at the organ. The Rev. John Gunnell, in the curse of an address, said he knew their dear friend Luke Ellison intimately for nearly twenty years and he shared with those who hid known him very much longer a high opinion of his character. Luke Ellison always seemed to posse is those sterling qualities that commanded the esteem of all goad men, whatever their political views or their religious sentiments and connections might be. He was honoured because he was honourable, and trusted because he was trustworthy. He o sed very little, perhaps nothing at all, to birth, educational facilities, and social standing, but lie, owed every- thing, humanly speaking, to character and to a wise and diligent use of the opportunities ha had in life. The outward facts of his life were very interest- ing. They had from his own pen an article that appeared in one of their local prints only a few weeks ago. Like many of the children of working people sixty and seventy years ag 3, he received the larger part of his education in the Sunday soh sof, (tad to the Sunday SOTIcol institution, wherever he was, he was vary closely attaohed, either as scholar, teacher, or offiae bearer, almost t ) the end of his life. He loved Sunday schools and he deli slated in Sunday school work because he felt his own obligations to the Sunday sohool. He was a lover of music, and he (Hr. Gunnell) could picture him now, standing at the end of the pew in the centre block, about half way down that Church, not with his hymn book simply, but with his hymn and tune book, following the psalmody of the services with great heartiness. At an early age he espoused the Temperance cause, and established a Good Templars' Lodge which, under his fostering care, became a very numerous and useful organization in the neighbourhood. He told them in his paper that he took a very deep interest in municipal work from his youth. He was a man of care- ful ol,servation and he saw how much the Welfare and happiness of the population— the clean'iness of the people's homes, the health of their bodies, the sweetness of the streets—depended on the administration of local affairs. It was only late in life, however, that he took a very active part in that administration. He (Mr. Gunnell) thought he was justified in assuming that 'their friend took his fair share of Council work—loyal to his colleagues and stead- fast in the discharge of the duties allotted to him. The latest honour which Came to him—that of eleotion as alderman—was greatly prized by him and be (the speaker) remembered how their friend received his congratulations with great cheerfulness and thankfulness "I want to say very emphatically" continued Mr. Gunnell "that before all else, Lutes Ellisan was a Christian man. All that I have said is perfectly con- sistent with a Christian course, but I want us all to see that at the very basis of his life, the inspiring motives, the lofty ideals that he cheriped were emphatically Christian in their character. An enlightened Christian conscience was his guide and his guardian angel in all social and political work. For well nigh half a century he was a member of our Methodist Church and during the greater part of that time a trusted officer—and we never found the trust misplaced Although in recent years he had retired from several offices that involved a great deal of thought and labour, his attendance on public worship never relaxed and his interest in everything that pertained to this Church and the school adjoining never lost its freshness. No news was more welcome to him than the news of prosperity in the Zion that he loved. lam not here to claim for Luke Ellisoa any great brilliancy of parts. He was not a highly cultured man, he was not, as I understand it, a magnetic man—not a born leader as some men are, but he was what is far better, he was a good man. Tare is no difficulty in these days in finding educated, cultured, and even magnetic men, but it is as hard as ever it was to find really good men— men of sterling character; men of high principle, men of stainless reputation—and our dear friend was all that. He was modest but never cringing, independent but never insolent. He had formed very strong opinions, he held very deep convictions, but he had toleration of the opinions and convictions of others and be was prepared to accept as among his closest friends those who differed from him on political and social questions.That,ss I bare read itovas the character of him whose remains we are committing to_ the dust. For seventy years he has been moving in and out among you serving his God and serving his generation by the will of God, and now like a tired labourer, he has gone to sleep. I believe his very last wOrds were I am going home.' He fell into a sweet sleep and in that sleep he died. We simply bow our head in humble reverence before Him who is toe wise to err and too good to be unkind." The ssrvice concluded with the " Dead troh "in " Saul."

The Mayor and Corporation and a d3tachment of police, in charge of Chief Inspector Williamson, headed the proces-ion as far as the Borough bounduy and then returned to town. There was another large and repre- sentative gathering at the service at Grappenhall Church, the Rev. G. C. Briggs, vicar of Christ Church, Latchford, officiating in the Church and at the grave- side.

Fiddlers' Ferry Sailing_ Club.

The club held its opening race a the season on Saturday from Fiddlers' Ferry to New Ferry. There was a stiff S.E. breeze. The six boats competing had a splendid start. Thelma was first over the line, closely followed by lone, Seagull, Hilda, Maggie, and Nina, only 20 seconds separating first and last boat, and finishing in the following order after a pleasant race :—lone (Thompson and Knowles), 4hrs. 48min. 50sec. ; Maggie (S. WARRINGTON HORSE PARADE.

New Arrangeinants.

Though Warrington has no organized May Day festivities, the" merrie month" is always chesen for the annual parade of horses, and this interestin g event wal, take place on Saturday next. Admirabl e arrangements hate been made and it is hoped that the parade will surpass any yet hel d as several new departures have been made with the object of initialing a larger number of enteies. The final meeting of the C emmittee te arrange details was held at the Lion Hotel on Tuesday. Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., presided, and amongst others present were :—Nlessrs. %V. Fletcher, Conncillor Fulton-Smith, J. M. Geeenhalgh, E. Draper, W. Bainbridge, S. Pickton, George Venn ani A. H. Clive (hon. secretary). The objeats of the parade are to improve,the general conditions and treatment of horses in the Berough, to encourage drivers to take a humane interest in the animals under their care, to encourage the use of horses suitable and fit for heavy work in the streets. Hitherto it has been the c astom to divide the parade into classes and therefore the woelcingman has had to eompete against the very large owper who has been able to place in the field very expensive and well-groomed horses. This *year the classes will be entirely done away with and each man whom the juiges °wielder worthy of a prize will receive one, quite independent of class. There is consequ may eve y encouragement to small owners to enter. For the convenience of the marshals the parade will be divided into sectioas. The marshals will be as follows :—Chief marshal, Mr. T. H. Pretty ; section a (light horse turnouts), Messrs. W. Bainbridge and B iinbridge, jun. ; b (Messrs. G. canal!, Whitley and Co.'s entries), Messrs. H. Ferguson and J. M. Greenhalgh; c (Warrington Slate Campany), Messrs. W. Fletcher and E. Isherwaod ; d (Warringtan Carporation), Mess Ts. S.Ticton and R. G at'!); e (Messrs. Peter Walker & Sans, Ltd.,) Ar. W. Pater Rylatids ; f (heavy miscellane- ous), alessri. Thomas Baxter and T. Williams ; g (non. competitors) ; Messrs. Wm. Faircloagh and U. Bu rrows Arraegameets are being made for diplomas ad merit badges to be presented by the Royal Society for the prevention of Critelty to Animals. Merit badges will fbe given to those drivers who are the winners of four diplomas, not necessarily in successive years or as driver of the same horse or to the same owner. The parade is now being conducted on the lines of the London Carriage Horse and Van Horse Par 4d5 acid the judges act as judges of the London shows. They are : The Iron. A. E. Parker. Mr. F. H. Norton Cttrlien, Warwick: Cayain E. W. Griffiths. Tiresfori, Ta rporle y ; Mr. C. W. Wilson, Rigmadon Park, Kirby Lonsdale Mr. It. Fleming, North Auelley-street, Landau, W. This year the judging will take plane, by kind per- mission of the Mayor, in sfront of the Town Hall. Competitors will assemble at 12 noon and approach the Town Hall gates, eastern side. BOLD STREET WESLEYAN SALE OF WORK.

Bold-street Wesleyan schoolroom Wai on Thursday the scene of a sale of Work in connection with the church adjoining. A large number of people witnessed the opening ceremony, the attendance including the Revs. Thomas Miller,. John Gunnell, Messrs. John Taylor, G. W. Weaver, W. Clegg, Percy Silcock, J. C. Holland, 11 H. Meldrum, Janes Lingard, F. A. Harris, Frank A. Hickman, J. T. Patterson, J. B. Doncaster, Edward Plinston, Mrs. Tort, Misses Toft, and Miss B. Dutton. _ _

Mrs. Toft declared the sale ()Pen; and Mr. J. Taylor presided. _ _

The Rev. THOMAS MILLER prior to placing the management of the proceedings in the hands of Mr. Taylor said it gave him great pleasure to see so many there. They believed that what they had set out to get would be accomplished, and they were glad that in the accomplishment of that aim the honours would fall upon those who were "associate I with Bold-street. They were very glad thr t Mrs. Toft and Mr. Taylor were there. For the work they had both done during the last few years they bath deserved and received honour. Continuing be said it was no use beating about the bush, they had met there that afternoon to raise £l5O. They wanted to clear off any debt there might be on the trust account. They wanted to clear any debt they had got on the church, and to mike some alterations to their" Westminster Abbey"—the men's Bible classroom. There should be some enlargement so that there would be plenty of room for the Bible class and also for,meetings in connection with the church. Mr. Taylar said he was very pleased to be present on that occasion—more so because his old friend Mrs. Toft, who had been reneighbour and friend for 30 years, was there. When there were any financial difficulties in connection with Bold-street they had such a lot of ladies who were trying to urge on the sales of work, and who had a strong desire to help, that they had no fear of failure. Then) were ladies there whom he met 35 years ago, and who were still doing the same kind of wk in connection with the sale of work. They had a noble a-my of workers and they deserved a little bit of help from outsiders. Their friends were giving as much today as their ancestors, but their expenditure was greater, and i n order to meet it they had to have sales of work. Was their, position spiritually as good to-day as it was 30 years ago.? Ife seemed to think that their Christianity was hardly adapted to the present age. He did not know why, but it was so. He did not think that the half-empty chapels were the faults of the ministers—be thought they preached as good sermons as they did 30 years ago. The cure for that apathy was really in the spiritual life of the Church. They needed a spiritual life that should rise even above all temptations. There was at the present time an extraordinary amount of pleasure going on in the world, and that pleasure seemed to ,be growing with young people, and it seemed to him that if they ban such a fascination for the pleasures of life, they did not like to take on the responsibilities of the Church. Mrs. Tat had handed to him £lO 10s. as her contribution. (Applause.) He could only say "Go and do likewise, and then we shall be in clover." (Applause). Mrs. Torr, prior to declaring the sale open, said she sincerely thanked the friends for inviting her to open the sale of work. S'ae was strongly attached to the Church. (Applause). On the proposition of Mr. G. W. Weaver, seconded 11 Mr. F. A. Harris a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to the chairman and Mrs. Toft. The staliholders were as follows : ,

SEWING MEETING STALL.—Mrs. hiier (president), Mrs. Weaver (vice-president), Mrs. Plinston (secretary). CONGREGATIONAL STALL.—Mrs. Guest, Mrs. Naden, Mrs. William Isaac, Mrs. I. E. Barlow. Mrs. Prince, YOUNG Max's STALL.—Mrs. J. C. Holland, Mrs. R. 11. Sutton, Mrs. G. A. Leach, Mrs. Whitmore. Miss Barlow, Miss Wilde, Mr. G. Shaw, Mr. A. S alder. FLOWER STALL.—Mrs. A.. 1. Silcock.

REFRESHMENT STALL.—Mrs. Harris, Mrs.S.P.Silcock. Tho entertainments were in the bands of Mr. Frank Hickman.

The proceeclto of the sale on Thursday amounted to£ll4. The silo was continued last evening. and a sAtis- factory sum realised.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME,

There is no very striking turn .at the Palace and Hippodrome this week, but the bill contains numerous items which are entertaining and amusing. The Olmars introduce novelty into the acrobatic business by combining poses in imitation of Roman statutory with their tumbling feats, and their efforts certainly deserve the applause awarded. Pat Clancy, as sub- stitute, created quite a furore with his wonderful quick-step dancing and " legomania." Norah Desmond, the American song illustrator, present some lint pictures in. colours and sings very tunefully. A clever gymnastic turn of the lightning variety is given by the Ramlos, whose smart feats on the horizontal bars are mingled with burlesque which never fail to raise a laugh. A very tasteful musical turn is that of Webb and Branston, operatic duettists, whose voices blend harmoniously in several popular items. A "mystic musical comedietta " centred round a supposed magical Indian bottle is presented by Harry Dent and Fanny Harris, who cram a lot of pantomime work, sinoing and buffoonery into then: allotted spell on the boards. The comedietta is not great by any means, but the artistes amuse—which is the chief thing ! Charles Vincent, comedian, sings funny songs in a stroll.. Cockney accent which would do with diluting. Mr. Widdop's orchestra submits some capital selections and the biograph has new and original pictures, .those depicting_ Alpine sports being splendid, Last evening the Perfection Soap Works Band was in attendance at both houses and played selections from Verdi's works which were enthusiastically received.

===== 0002601_19090515_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME,

An exceptionally attractive programme is announced for next week, the top place •being given to Fred Ginnett and Wal Pink's romantic sensation Claude Duval." Eileen Douglas, refined comedienne, will provide another of the leading turns, and Maurin's marvellous fountains will be something unique and pleasing from the spectacular point of view. Other attractions will be :—Roy Ford, comedienne; Cover- dale and Iluxley's Ji Jou Juvenile troupe; Johnson and Bert in "A queer customer ;" Burns and Bently, comedians and dancers.

By controlling our manufactories we place the goods on the Market bearing one profit only—the goods going direct from us, the manufacturers, to our customer, the consumer—bearing only the one profit and not two or three as charged elsewhere.
